An American poultry dealer offered a prize of £5 to my customer who could guess the number of feathers on a chicken chosen from his stock. A gentleman won with 8,120.

A two nights' debate on Christianity v. Secularism was opened in the Cairo Street Schools on Tuesday evening, when the question under discussion was " Was Jesus Christ a wise and moral teacher ?"—Councillor Stott presided over a good attendance. Mr. J. W. RIISIITQN, of Leeds, of the Church of Latter Day Saints opened the discussion in the affirmative. The question they had to consider was' Did the evidences of reason and probability lead them to believe that the teach- ings of Christ as applied to human life and conduct would produce the highest life and character ?" He believed that they would. In the Commandment "Thou shalt love Thy God with all thy heart, and thy neighbour las thyself" Christ in his selection of the doctrine of I love displayed the intensest wisdom and morality I which had never been surpassed by any other teacher of morals and wisdom. When He said ' Blessed are the poor in spirit for their's is the kingdom of Heaven" Christ was in direct detachment from the world which in evervthing sought for power and riches, and in the . ; seeking brought about wars both material and com- mercial. To produci anything approximating universal I peace man must be poor in spirit and must have God in I his heart. In the text "Blessed are the meek for, they shall inherit the earth" Christ selected the ! only true course and pathway to real peace, rest and success. Nothing taught by Jesus 1 Christ was in conflict with the scientific oasis of morality,in fact there was something in his teaching which was above the scientific laws. The acceptance of His teaching would result in a universal federation and amalgamation of men and women and " Man's inhumanity to men" would become an anachronism. (Applause.) I Mr. PERCY WARD leader of the Liverpool Secular Society, followed in he negatlve. Christ taught" He that believeth not shall be damned." Fear, as taught in that text, would he contended make moral slaves of men and in that Christ was neither moral nor wise. Christ Himself said He was not come to bring peace but s sword, and taught that hatred of family and friends was necessary to entering the kingdom of God. He taught distinctly the doctrine of everlasting torment as the result of unbelief, and how could that be recon- ciled with the fatherhood of God ? The first three , essentials for humanity were food, clothing and shelter, and yet Christ taught that no thought should be taken for these things. When He taught "Take no thought for the morrow "Christ was not wise. If that teaching were followed crops would be neglected and in a few years starvation and death would face humanity. (Applause.) Both speakers had further periods allotted to them, in the course of one of which Mr. Ward asked how the horrors of San Francisco could be reconciled with the idea of Vie loving fatherhood of God. Mr. Rushton replied that such terrible disasters were mysteries

which they could not fathom, but doubtless when they could see the plan of God in one harmonious whole they would be able to see His wisdom and love even there. At the same time, knowing that the, district was subject to seismic distur.Rtuces, the prople should have taken warning and not have run the risk. A good hearing was given to each speaker and both were a pplauded. At the close a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to the Chairman.

BLOOD IMPURITIES.

A Spring Trouble Cured by

Bile Beans.

A disordered liver is the primary cause of impure blood. If the liver is not working properly it is unable to perform its functions, among the chief of which is the purification of the blood. Impure blood poisons the whole of the system, and skin eruptions are common results of the upheaval which follows. Bile Beaus, by correcting the disordered liver, expel from the system. all impure matter ; they also so stimutate the digestive organs as to ensure that all possible blood creating matter is extracted from the load taken.

Mr. Alexander Warton of 90, Brinkburn-street, Byker,Newcastle, says :—" lam nineteen years of age, I and have, thanks entirely to Bile Beans, recently been rid of skin eruptions which for four years had caused me great annoyance Rd discomfort. lam a member ' of a local footbalr club; and it was after a game that I .first noticed tie spots ori each side of my mouth. The spots spread rapidly, and soon my whole face was one mats of burning, irritating pimples. Water used to- run from my eyes and course down my cheeks,and often I have been asked what I was crying for. " All the remedies I tried proved useless until some months ago a friend at work advised me tp try Bile ll•3ans. My appetite had by this time dwindled con- siderably. I tried half-a-dozen t 'ens which my friend gave roe, and these had such a hneficial effect that I 'immediately obtained more. After a short course of Bile Beans the pimples had all vanished, and my face sts as fresh and clear complexioned as ever. My appetite has returned again, and I feel better than have done for years. I shall always be pleased to recommend Bile Beans." -

Bile Beans are the ideal spring-time blood purifier. Sold only in sealed boxes, of all chemists, or post free from the Bile Bean Manufacturing Co., 4, Red-cross- street, London, E.C., at Is. I.A. and 2s. 91. (larger size contains three times as many beans as the Is. I,lcl. size).

EDUCATION COMMITTEE. THE SELECTION OF REPRESENTATIVE MEMBERS. _ " Scene " Between Councillor Pemberton and Mr. H. Roberts. The monthly meeting of the Warrington Education Committee was held at the Town Hall on Monday, when there were present :—Alderman Bennett (in the chair), the Mayor; Aldermen Smethurst, Tinnion and Evans ; Councillors Fulton Smith, J. G. Craik, S /key, Ellison, Lord, Burton, Barton, Dr. Joseph, Wilkinson, and James Evans; Revs. Canon Morley Stevenson, N. Wilson John' Yonge,and J. Davies; Messrs. H. Kingdon, •C. E. Wilson, E. Plinston, C. S. Burg. IS, R. T. Fair- dough, H. Roberts, C. H. Unsworth, G. Walker, and J. Upson; Mr. J.Ploore Murray (director of education), and Mr. J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer). THE STAFFING OF SCHOOLS. The CHAIRMAN announced that with regard to the committee's recent resoletion on the question of the improved staffing of schools there seemed to bea strong feeling among some members of the Town Council at the last meeting that as there had been some change in the personnel of the School Management Committee the matter might receive further coraideratlon. He therefore withdrew the resolution and the a hole ques- tion would be considered by the new committee. Mr. R. T. Faramonoxsaid he would like to call the attention of the committee to the fact that the National Schools were not ea well staffed as they were before the Act came into force. The CHAIRMAN said that matter hardly came within within the scope of the resolution. The whole question of staffing schools would be recorsidered by the School Management Committee. REPRESENTATIVE MEMBERS. NAMES TO BE SUBMITTED TO THE COUNCIL'. LIVELY SCENE. The CHAIRMAN said that at the last meeting of the Council he withdrew the committ:3's recommendation that Mr. A. J. Jolley and Mrs. Edelsten should I appointed representative members. His reason for doing so was that some rnembars of the Council took technical exception to the fact that the matter was only brought up at the close of the last meeting of the Education Committee and WPS not on the agenda. The only motive for bringing the question forward then was to accelerate the completion of the committee. The DIRECTOR read a letter from Mrs. Edelsten, who was asked to allow her name to be nominated, stating that she took it as a considerable compliment that the Education Committee should desire her to be one of its members. She did not kuow whether the committee had to submit names again to the Cancil but she would ask them not to include her's. In the present acrimonious state of affairs she thought a woman's name should not be involved. The CHAIRMAN said he was sure they would all regret to receive the letter but they had no alternative but to accept it. Mr. PLINSTON proposed the nomination of Mr. Thomas May who was a very useful member and in his work on the School Attendance Committee had been very painstaking and attentive. Councillor BARTON seconded. In anawer to Alderman Tinnier, the Chairman said the Council seemed to think the Committee should recommend a lady for election to the Committee. It would,however, rest with the Committee to make what recommendation it saw fit and the Council must take the responsibility of accepting or rejecting it. Alderman TINNION said that if the matter were left to that committee he believed it would be settled in a reasonable and common sense manger He moved the nomination of Mr. A. J. Jolley in whom he had every conedence, believing him to have given of his best in the interests of education in Warrington. Mr. Jolley had devoted his time, ability and experience to the work and it would be almost a calamity if the Com- mittee lost his services. Mr. C. B. PARKER seconded. He understood there was some objection to the name of Mr. Jolley, and the Town Council were anxious that the Committee should recommend an (a Jnomist. He claimed that Mr. Jolley was one of the best economists they had, He quite admitted that Mr. Jolley went for economy through efficiency, but bethought that all who were engaged in commercial concerns believed that was the best way of going for economy. On the School Management Com- mittee, whenever anything approaching extravagance was suggested Mr. Jolley was one of the first to put his foot upon it. He believed Mr. Jolley knew more about the details of the education question than any of those present. Mr. UNSWORTH supported. It would undoubtedly be a distinct loss to the town if Mr. Jolley were not elected. I Councillor LORD supported the nomination'of Mr. May whom he knew to be an economist. He did not. I want to say anything disrespectful about Mr. Jolley but this was the first time he had heard him spoken of as an economist. Mr. Jolley did not appeal to him as an economist. Councillor WILKINSON, in supporting Jr. Jolley said that apart from what any members of the Council might think they had against him there was 0110 good feature of Mr. Jolley and that was that he was a willing and a hard worker. If they thought Mr. Jolley had not been an economist, it was for the coruittee as it was newly constituted to cee that he was an economist in future. Councillor PEMBERTON said he felt sure the Council would send back any resolution of the Committee unless one of the names proposed was that of a lady. Con- tinuing he said he should support the nomination of Mr. May who wes an economist. He was sorry to have to refer to the matter he was about to mention, but as Mr. Jolley's friends had pressed his name forward so much be would not be doing his duty in the interests of the public if he did not mention it. Some four years ego Mr. Jolley put up for election to the Town Council and the ratepayers rejected him. He (Councillor Pemberton) believed the great reason why they did BO was because he was not an economist,but had alreputation for being a spender. Since 'he had been elected, to the Education Committee Mr. Jolley had I been before the public again and the electors would net have him. Why some of his friends should want to force him on to a public body he (Councillor Pemberton) could net eay. He had no personal feeling against Mr. Jolley who was a very nice gentleman to know as a friend. But those who were working for economy in public business felt it very much to have a man thrust on them like Mr. Jolley who,they contended, had no idea!of the value of money.( •Oh")It was said that Mr. Jolley was a good worker and gave a great deal of time to the duties, but it was dear time to the public of Warrington. Then they must look at Mr. Jolley's work on the Board of Guardians and in other capacities. There was no doubt the public would not elect him if he had to come before them. ("Question.") Until Mr. Jolley was returned by the public, they must assume that they did not want him. They wanted value for every pound they spent, and Mr. Jolley in his (Councillor Pemberton's) opinion, and in that of a great many people, did not look as much to the economical side of the question as he 'ought to do. Mr. H. ROBERTS said he considered the remarks of the last speaker ac applied to Mr. Jolley to he nothing more or less than a grass libel. For a man to get up and say he had no personal feeling against Mr. Jolley, when, unfortunately for him, they knew his past record was rather asking too much. Councillor PEMBERTON : I object to those remarks. MT. ROBERTS : You can object. Councillor PEMBERTON : There is nothing in my past record that ought to be brought forward in that manner I strongly object to that. I shan't allow that to go on. The CHAIRMAN: It is for me to say what I will allow to go on. I hope Mr. Roberts— Councillor PEMBERTON : What is my past record ? Mr. H. ROBERTS : It will take more than this night to describe it. The CHAIRMAN : I ion't think anything can be gained by such personalities. Councillor PEMBERTON : I object to Mr. Roberts's remarks and lam astonished at you allowing them or remarks about anyone else's past record here. The CHAIRMAN: You were speaking of Mr. Jolley's past record. Councillor PEMBERTON : lan talking about public work. Mr. ROBERTS : am talking about public work and your past record. The CHAIRMAN: If Mr. Roberts says anything glaringly unfair I will stop him. Mr. RoBERTs : The attack made on Mr. Jolley was entirely on his past record as a public man and I say it comes with very ill ',trace from a public man with a record like Councillor Pemberton. The CHAIRMAN : It is as well to leave that alone. Mr. ROBERTS : I have said it. Councillor PEMBERTON : I ask the Chairman to ask Mr. Roberts to withdraw/that. Mr. Roberts : I shall riot withdraw it. Councillor PEMBERTON : I explained what I objected to. . ---

Mr. ROBERTS : And I have explained what T mean. Councillor PEMBERTON : I referred to Mr. Jolley's public performances in regard to economy. The CHAIRMAN: 'Mr. Roberts has not said anything detrimental to your character. I don't know what he means by your past record. Mr. ROBERTS : Councillor Pemberton knows what I mean. I leave him to settle it in his own way. con- tinning he said they were dealing with Mr. Jolley as a fit and proper person to represent the question of educa- tion and it was most unfair to bring up against his candidature for that position anything which might to said against him rightly or wrongly in regard to his conduct in anotherposition which had no connection whatever with that committee and whoT e.l the conditions were absolutely different. These economists had 1 an objection to Mr. Jolley and they said he was a spendthrift. They could not do it and they knew they could not. Councillor PEAIBERTON : Yes they can. Mr. Remus : And all this nonsense about hita being d *

I say without' hesitation that there is not a single man in this town who is as well up—as a non- professional man—upon the whole question of the administration of the Education law as Mr. Jolley is. I will say more. There is not ,itiother man, not a soli- tary one, taking everything into cc:count, who has made the sacrifices and given the tithe actuated by the one supreme desire to do what in him lies for the benefit of the children of this town to a greater extent than hae Mr. Jolley. To accuse him of being a spendthrift is a falsehood.

The CliarnmAN : You must not use a word like that.- Mr. ROBERTS Well I will say it is not strictly accurate. (Laughter.) It is a terminological inexacti- tude, I suppose—an up-to-date expression for des- cribing what we used to describe in a shorter' and more emphatic manner. The application of the term " spendthrift " to Mr. Jolley is another' illustration of the disposition of men in these times to use terminological inexactitudes. (Laughter.) Con- tinuing Mr. Roberts said that both Mr. Jolley and Mr. May were conscientious men, anxious for the well- being of the town, but he felt it was no use recommend- ing the names of two gentlemen to the Council. It was not consistent with the dignity of the Council or of that committee that they should be playing a game of shuttlecock over the two names. Let them in the interests of the cause they professed to be trying to serve, drop the personal element. If they had to choose between Mr. Jolley and Mr. May he had no difficulty in making his choice, much as he would regret that the committee and the town would loses the services offiMr. May. He was sorry Mrs. Edelsten had thought it necessary, although he was not surprised, to insist upon her name being withdrawn. As she declined to accept the position under present conditions he sug- gested the name of Miss Williams, head of the Priory School, who was possessed of very high educational qualifications and was deeply interested in educational work.

Councillor Dr. JOSFPH seconded. Miss Williams, he said, was very highly educated, having graduated at London University in arts,and he thought no lady could be better qualified to become a member of the com- mittee.

Mr. UPSON bore testimony to Mr. Jolley's services as a representative of the committee on various import- ant authorities. The Rev. JOHN YONGE said that having sat with Mr. Jolley for three years on the School Management Cora- mittee he knew him to be the foe of extravagance. He esteemed Mr. Jolley'very highly and was sure he was perfectly sincere in all he did for the education of the borough. He believed that those gentlemen who thought he was a spendthrift were altogether in error. Tn regard to Miss Williams, he considered her to be a thoroughly fit and competent person to serve on the Committee.

The IVIAVOR. said he believed Mr. Jolley had acted conscientiously in all he had done. He did not suppose Mr. Jolley if elected would be placed on the School Management Committee as he only committee on which there was a vacancy Was theyinance Committee. He (the Mayor) should vote for Mr. Jolley and Mr. May, believing the latter to be a well meaning gentle- man although at times he brought forward some peculiar ideas.

The Committee decided that one of the persons re- commended should be a lady. On a vote' being taken on the names of Mr. Jolley and Mr. May, Mr. Jolley was selected by 21 votes to 7. The names of Mr. Jolley and Miss Williams will therefore be submitted to the Council. SCHOOL CARETAKER'S WAGES. The Finance Cammittes amongst other things recom- mended-that Ruth Blenkinsop he appointed caretaker of the Ladies' School of Industry, at a wage of 12s. per week. Mr. E. PLINSTON moved that the minute be referred back. A certain person bad been doing the work for £52 a year, am' her daughter, who was to succeed her, was to receive 12s. a week. As there was still the same amount of work to be donAhe would like to know the reason for the reduction. Mr, R. T. PAIRCLOUGH said the reason was that the school was a small one and the committee thought that in the past they had been paying too much for the work The late Mrs. Blenkinsop moreover was a widow and her daughter was a married woman. - Councillor LORD seconded the amendment on the ground that the proposed payment was contrary to the scale. Mr. UPSON supported. It was, he thought, poor sort of economy which cut a wage of £1 - down to 12s. The MAYOR supported the !committee. The arnellnitt paid for cleaning in some cases was excessive an° b. the committee could get the work done for 12s. a wee' they ought to do so. On a vote being taken Mr. Plinston's amendment was carried. TEACHER'S LEAVE OF ABSENCE. HEADMASTER'S REPORT. The School Management Committee recommowlef three month's leave of absence , that the headmistress of St. Peter's school be granted her to be paid according to scale—in full for the first month and one half during the Second month—and that a temporary assistant be engaged during her absence. salary Canon STEVENSON, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said there seemed to have been an impression in the minds of some members of the Committee that that they had been granting leave of absence of teacherei at the expense of the rates. That was not so. The Committee hitherto had granted leave of absence for ill-health, and had allowed the schools to be carried OR for the time being by the remainder of the staff. There had beeh no extra expense incurred, as the salaries paid were exactly the same as they were before, the only difference being that one teacher had been away, and the rest had got on as well as they could without. The School Management Committee felt that that was not altogether satisfactory, and had taken steps that in the future when any serious leave of absence was allowed to appoint a supply teacher. On the minutes of the committee was a resolntior, with regard to a request from the Warrington Branch of the National Union of Teachers asking that a/I- reporl by headmasters should be shown to the teachere concerned. The committee reselved "That, when it iS proposed by the Committee to withhold an increase of salary, otherwise due, from any teacher owing to the, receipt of an unsatisfactory report from the head teacher or the managers of the school where such teacher is engaged, the teacher so reported upon shire have an opportunity of stating his or her case before. this Committee prior to the final decision of the Com- mittee with regard to each advance of salary." Referring to this minute Canon STEVENSON said the Committee had every reason to hope that the resolution would very largely meet the objections of the teachers, and that it would work without friction. The Rev. JOHN TONGS seconded. Mr. C. IL UNSWORTri asked what salary teachers de leave of absence would receive during the third month. Alderman BENNETT : Nothing. Mr: UNswonTrt desired to call the attention of the committee to the fact that at their last meeting their Mayor made a very strong appeal to the Committee on the ground that leave of absence was costing the town Rtioo a year. It had been shown that the leave of absence actually cost the= town nothing. Then the' so-called economists_were put upon the School Manage- ment Committee, and went a step flintier and incurred expense. Ile could not understand their inconsistency. The MAYOR said it appeared as though the schools Lad been over-staffed. (" No, no.") When a less number of teachers could do the work it looked verf much like it, and it certainly appeared as though there. was one teacher too many. Under the new arrange- ment the ratepayers would have to pay more, but the proposal would bare a tendency to stop leave of absence- Councillor WIT.KINSON thought the new arrangement as compared with the old one meant the difference. between economy with efficiency and economy without efficiency. The resolution would have the effect of dis- covering which persons honestly deserved leave of absence end which did not. Councillor CRAIR moved that a minute recommending the appointment of the chairman of the committee, Councillor Evans and Councillor Wilkinson as a depu- tation to attend the International Congress on School Hygiene, to be held in London, he referred hack. He thought it sufficient if the chairman of the committee attended the congress. Mr. ROBERTS, referring to the leave of absence pro- posal, thought that, as the members of the Corporation were such strict economists, whenever any proposal incurrin,g increased exdenditure came from them, it should he unanimously agreed to. WOMEN'S CHAT.

Lady Burdett-Contts.—The Baroness Burdett-Contts, -who last week celebrated her ninety-second birthday is really a wonderful woman. The lady who was born before Waterloo, who remembers the Coronation of William TV., was bridesmaid to Queen Victoria, who numbered among her friends the Duke of Wellington, Levi ()rev. Sir Robert Peel, and Lord Palmerston, still manifests a lively interest in public affairs, and only the other day received a boy who had saved a com- rade s life at sea, and decorated him with the Humane Society's medal. Until recent, years, the Baroness was a regular attendant on first nights at the theatre, and never missed the private view at the Royal Academy, but she still takes a carriage drive almost every day, and is always accompanied on these occasions by one or more of her favourite dogs. Much of her time is spent at her house in Stratton-street, Piccadilly. It has only quaintly old-fashioned furniture, but contains many priceless treasures and cedes, although few are more prized than those which are the simple presentations of the poor. The Baroness married M. Burdett-Coutts in 1881, and this year celebrrited her silver wedding. dust a hundred years ago—in 1806—her father Sir Francis Burdett, will" returned to Parliament for Westminster, for whicE constituency her husband is now member. '; COMELY WOMAN."

A little work which will prove of particular inter Est to the fair sex is" Comely Woman ' by John Strange Winter who as a powerful writer ranks high in the estimation of the book-loving public. The book, which is brightly and lucidly written, has-for its theme "The charm of that quality which we call cotnliness," and the writer imparts golden rules which cannot fail to be of the highest value in the cultivation of that quality by which she rightly sets such store. The secrets of a good appearance—a valuable asset in the struggle for existence and a large factor in the pleasures of life— are, it is pointed out, good toilet habits, and the book abounds in useful hints as to the care of the hair and the complexion. The subject is dealt with in a very exhaustive but extremely interesting way and the hook is certainly worthy of the careful perusal of those who, as the authoress puts it, would be comely. A copy will be sent post free on application to "John Strange Winter," 11, West Kensington Mansions, London, W.

The Examiner, Saturday, April 28, 1906. WOMAN'S REALM. , SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Wycliffe Church.

The Rev. John Yonge preached to good congregations at this church on Sunday. In the morning he based his sermon on the words " The thief cometh not, but for to steal and to kill, and to destroy. I am come that they might have life and that they might have ft more abundantly." At the evening service his subject was " Thomas,the doubting disciple."

The sudden downpour of rain which, usually follows a bright flash of lightning has generally been supposed to be the result of the .rlischarge of the electricity. Advanved weather students now put forward the statement that the con- trary is the case; in' other words, that the sudden increased precipitation causes the light- ning flash, instead of the lightning flash caus- ing the sudden ,incrcase in rainfall.

THE COMPOSITION OF ALBUMEN.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe P.S.A.

Mr. Hilton presided over this meeting on Sunday last and gave a most interesting and encouraging address. He touched upon some happy experiences in P.S.A. work at Blackburn and Morecambe and other places where men who had not been reached by the ordinary Church services but had been attracted by the P.S.A.'had become very useful workers.—The Rev. J. F. Matthews spoke in his usual style and made some telling remarks upon the terrible calamities that have taken place during the last few years in distant lands. Now we had the great eruption and the terrible earth- quake, proving the great uncertainty of life and the certainty of death. If we were accustomed to hard, loving and faithful service and consistent life, we were then always prepared for living or dying.—The Rev. J. Yonge welcomed one new member.—Mr. A. Downes read the lesson and Mr. Yonge led the prayer.—Mr. Payton, Mus. Bac., presided at the organ and gave an organ solo which delighted the membars.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
BOtlst Church, Colborne Street.

The Rev. T. Dunnico occupied the pulpit at the above church on Sunday last both morning and evening. In the morning be brought to a close a series of sermons on Christian Verities by discussing the doctrine of the Trinity. Some people, the preacher said, thought the doctrine of the Trinity was an obsolete theological fact destined to pass away like the Gloctrine of hyper- Calvinism before the light and intelligence of modern days. Such, however, would not be the case. The doctrine was of permanent value, and whilst many narrow and crude interpretations of the fact would disappear, the fact would abide as truth ever did. He willingly confessed that there was much that was mysterious, but mystery was not necessarily inconsistent or antagonistic taireason. All knowledge was relative, and mystpry was but a salutory reminder that man's powers wefe finite and that there were many things in heaven and earth not I dreamed of in their philosophies. The Trinity was the three-fold manifestation ofithe three-fold nature of God and had its origin in the three successive manifestations I God had given in history. In the evening the church was crowded and Mr. Dunnico preached upon "The city that was and is, and 'the city that is to be." Basing his remarks on Gen. iv. 17 and Rev. xxi. 2, the preacher said that in the Book of Genesis they had the city as it was and to a large extent the city as it is. but in the Book of Revelation they had the city as it would be in the daystthat were to be. Whilst there was much in the cities of to-day that evoked pride and bore testi- mony to the strength of human mind there was much in every city of pain, poverty and sin. These things were due to the prevalent spirit of ungod- liness, unrighteousness and unbrotherliness. The city of the future would however be the reverse of all this because permeated with the spirit of Christ which stood for Godliness, Brotherliness and Righteousness. In conclusion the preacher urged his hearers to do their utmost as individuals to usher in that city where there would be one law—love, one language—truth, one task —noble and affectionate service.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
Legh-street Primitive Methodist Chapel.

There were large congregations at this place of worship on Sunday when the Sunday school anniversary services were held. The special preacher for the day was the Rev. Danzy Sheen, ex-Sunday school secretary. The children rendered special hymns throughout the day under the conductorship of Mr. A. Spruce. the choirmaster. A musical service was held in the after- noon, and was well attended, the children and choir contributing special items. The soloists were Misses May Isherwood, and Rachnel Peacock and Master Harry Griffiths. Mr. F. Podmore presided at the organ. At the evening service the Rev. Danzy Sheen delivered an address on "The training of children," taking as his text "And ye fathers, provoke not your childien to wrath, but nurture them in the fear and admonition of the Lord." The Sunday school, said the speaker, was an excel- lent training ground for the children. The home life too, should cultivate in a child all that was morally heantiful. Fathers should take i an interest n their children, try to answer their ques- tions, and be an example that the child might copy. On the following evening a fair attendance assembled in the schoolroom to hear Mr. Sheen lecture on "John Bull and Uncle Sam ; the bond of their friendship." After citing several humorous instances regarding the peculiar traits of the Tpresentatives of the American and English nationalities, Mr. Sheen • gave a sum- ' trahaarti zgerde ahti s nt oart Toon! thhee said,Uni t ed were States.the PT hi l ger if mo u n Fathersde r s of, I who rather than sacrifice their loyalty to the Protestant religion sailed from Southampton in the Mayflower, to seek freedom and a home in the New World. For 150 Tears they and their descendants remained loyal subjects lOf Britain. Then, when an unwise Government ' threatened to place a duty upon their tea, the hitherto loyal colonists sturdily resisted and as no British volun- teers could be obtained to fight their kith and kin across the seas the British Government hired Hessian troops. The Americans triumphed and Washington was elected their first president. Referring to the bond of friendship which existed between the Englishman and American, the lecturer gave it as his firm conviction that although language and blood might cement the friendship, the strongest tie was the universal Christian religion, which laid the foundation of the great American nation, and which was also the religion of the Englishmen.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church.

Rev. Sidney Pitt and the Divinity of Christ.

The morning congregations at Hewsey-road Wes- leyan Church have been steadily improving of late, and there was a large attendance on Sunday when the Rev. A. Cecil Parker preached. His sermon was a most powerful one, being based on the words : "Have ye received the Holy Ghost since ye believed ?" (Acts xix., 2.) In the evening the Rev. Sidaey Pitt preached to a large congregation from the text : " And on His head were many crowns " (Revelation xix., 12.) The first Crown on the head of the Saviour was, he said, that of Christ's divinity. This Jesus who walked the earth inhuman form, a real man, was all the time Very God of Very God, the only begotten of the I Father, the eternal Son who was with the Father before the foundation of the world. Their friends sometimes sang" A crowning day is coming by and by," but there was a Crown that He had worn before the world was. And let them not suppose that this was a thing that did not matter. There were those, and many of them he feared amongst their young people, who were allowing themselves to think, without really taking the trouble to look into it, that Christ was after all not so very much different from a very good man, and there were even Cluistian teachers who spoke as if the differences between themselves and the Unitarians or even the more pious Jews were not of sufficient importance to really divide them. There was one in the past who sold his Master. There were nt a few just now who seemed ready to give Him away. Now whatever might be said about the true and proper divinity of ;our Lord, for or against it, for anyone to suppose that it did not matter was to betray shallowness of thought. Who Christ was and what He was mattered a great deal to both in relation to God and to themselves. Suppose that our Lord were but a man, only better and greater than other men, or suppose accordinr, to the far nobler conception of the ancient Arians, that he were the noblest of all creatures brought forth before the foundation of the world but a creature still, coming into being within the limits of time, then their whole conception of the 'divine Being would be altered and degraded. The God they would have to worship then would not be the same as the God they worshipped now. Think what they would have before the worlds were made or before such an exalted creature came into being. They would have a lonely God, and that would be the case during all the past eternity—a .God with none to contemplate, and none to love, and none to return His love. Sych a God would not be the Eternal Father whom they had learnt to,trust and love. He would be an altogether different Being -they could almost pity such an one. It was a conception from which they shrank with pain. But there never was this lonely ' God. The love of the eternal Father flowed forth through all eternity to the eternal Son. There in the bosom of the Father He dwelt through all eternity, and so, before the world was, far back before our thought could reach, the eternal God not only was love, but had as the eternal Father the object for His love.

DRSS-FASIIION-HOME.

GLIMPSES OF FASHION.

Black gowns, smartly • ma,d3 and trimmed, are old favourites, but each season they appear with some variation, says the Lady. Black and ; white striped velvet is Most effective when the I, gown is in corselet or Empire style, and thi fabric is used for wide cuffs and large lapels, also for buttons of varying size. Diamond- I shaped medallions. enclosed in scrolls of pleat- ing, are also. used, and the deep swathed belt ! is charming in black and white velvet. cut on the bias, ta be worn with a boler3 and skirt costume which is faced and trimmed with the same stripe. Chine and Pompadour silks in bright colourings form delightful trimmings for either black or tinted lace gowns. On black lace, motifs of the chine silk are enclosed in scrolls of ruching, quilling,;; or pleatings of silk or lace, and a sash or belt with little tails fanci- fully arranged is most effective under a loose bolero of black lace lined with chiffon. A crossed vest of chine silk, fastened with three or four fancy paste buttons, also has its charm, and is equally suitable for black or tinted lace.

CHARMING SPRING lIATB.

Pictured in our illustration are two charming spring hats, one suited for smart morning wear, the second as an accompaniment to more dressy toilets. The plainer hat is in the extremely fashionable mushroom shave. with- a deep curved-over brim (a delightfully shady affair in sunny weather), and low round crown. The straw of which the hat is composed is a rough, firm plait, in colour a warm biscuit. On the left side is arranged a large and jaunty bow of ribbon, patterned with a blue-and-green cheek

on a white ground. Similar ribbon is twisted round the crown, and forms the cachepeign• tinder the brim behind. The second hat, in a pretty tilted shape, is made, of soft, fine straw in a becoming shade of grey-green. Round the crown is placed a .f old of dark-green velvet rib- bon, and round this, again a wreath of cowslips. Similar velvet is tw Bre., into a large bow with hanging ends on the Itf t wide, and the hat is completed by a. bunch o. s; slips placed under the upturned brim.

WOMEN AND GAMBLING.

There is no vice, declares Ella Hepworth Dixon, so unbecoming to a woman, or one which alters the expression of her f 4,ee so largely or so permanently, as gambling This is so patent that a walk through the gambling rooms at Monte Carlo leaves one with the impression that to call the female sex beautiful is gross flattery, at least, if one studies the faces of the habitual gamblers. Nor, if they only knew it, do the majority of ladies playing bridge for high 4:awe look any more pleasing and agreeable. There are men who can lose fortunes in a night and not turn a hair, but carry off the loss of large sums with an imperturbable countenance, but this self-con- trol is rarely within the compass of the more emotional and sensitive sex. And not without reason, for a man when he gambles is usually los- ing his own money, whereas a woman in a like case is usually losing someone else's money—that of her husband or father. When it comes to young girls playing bridge for money the thing is preposterous. How a gentleman, or even an elderly woman, can pocket the money of a debu- tante—a child just out of the school-room—is one of those social mysteries which it is difficult to fathom. Cards in connection with gambling should be reserved as a last resource for the elderly, for those to whom all other amusements have beoome taskgess or forbidden. It should be considered exclusively a pastime for the old. Many thoughtful persons are in favour of a bill to prevent boys smoking tobacco before the age of eighteen or twenty. Why should not a kin- dred legal Act be brought in to prohibit all women under fifty from gambling at cards? The greet would be instantaneous and surprising.

A TJIMFIIL SHIRT BLOUSE.

As the season advances, the popularity of plain, tailor-made shirts increases by leaps and bounds. These shirts are made of all sorts of washing materials, from a heavy and expensive

linen to a zephy at 4id. the yard. The particu- lar example illustrated in our sketch is fashioned of a pretty striped zephyr—heliotrope stripes upon a white ground. These stripes are at-- ranged on the diagonal throughout,' the blouse. At the top bf the shirt is a deep-pointed yoke, piped with plain heliotrope, and machine-stitched. This yoke is crossed on either side by a band composed of a succession of stitched and piped bands, each finished with & crochet button. Down the middle of the front runs a boa-pleat, similarly piped and stitched and adorned with groups of the crochet buttons. The sleeves are of the bishop type, but instead of the ordinary narrow waistband have long doep cuffs, crossed with piped, stitched, and buttoned bands similar to those on tho blouse. The shirt is completed by a turn-down collar and a mauve-and-white plaid cravat.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

DEVILLED RABBIT.—Joint a voting rabbit and parboil the pieces. Set them aside to cool. Scoye them to the bone, making the gashes quite close together. Meantime melt two tablespoonfuls of butter and season with one-eighth of te teaspoon- ful of red pepper, half a teaspoonful of salt, half a teaspoonful Of mustard, one tabltspoofiful of vinegar or lemon juice, and one teaspoonful of Worcester sauce. Mix well and brush each piece of rabbit with it. taking Caro to rub it well into the gashes. Have the broiling chamber ot gas stove hot, and brown the rabbit, turning 'several times. Meantime keep the remainder of the devilling mixture and pour it over tø mbbit we; Serving. •

Professor Emil Fischer, of Berlin, is said to have accomplished the first complete analysis of natural albumen. Ho has established the compo- sition of various of its ingredients, and some of these he has produced artificially. By a combi- nation of these he has obtained what he calls polypeptide. This sxtbst!mc+-3 possesses a large number of the properties of natural albumen. If, as Pfeiffer has said, albumen is the source of all muscular strength, then the artificial production of this substance may be .of great importance.

ROCK SALT IMPERVIOUS. TO RADIUM.

In a German scientific paper, Elster and Geitel point out that rock salt acts as an efficient screen against radium radiations. In considerable thick- ness it even absorbs the very penetrative Y-rays. Its own radio-activity, atrain, is very slight. The authors have tested the efficiency of rock salt as a screen against radium emanations in a salt mine near Wolfenbiittel, and found that the rate of dissipation was reduced 28 per cent. It is in- teresting to note that while rock salt is thus shewn to be impervious to radiations from radium, it is almost the only substance which allows heat rays to pass through it.

THEORY OF STEEL TEMPERING.

M. Grenet, chief of the laboratories of the Saint Jacques works, in a communication to the Sosiete de 'lndustrie Alliterate, revsiews the theories that have attempted to explain the tem- pering of steel, one of tho most commonly ac- cepted being that it keeps the metal in an un- stable state while normally it is stable cold. He does not think that tempered steels owe their properties to the fact that they preserve the stable state hot, but-because they undergo at a coeffparatively low temperature the conversion that brings them to the stable state cold. He has studied carbon steels, special nickel steels, chrome-nickel steels, and manganese steels. For nickel steels, as for carbon steels, whenever the conversion takes place at a low temperature, the steel is hard, its microscopic texture is fine, and it remains magnetised after having been sub- mitted to a magnetic charge. This hardness and remaining magnetism may, according to him, be attributed either to the fine texture, due to the separation at low temperature of the ele- ments in a metal not sufficiently malleable for these elements to collect, or to a phenomenon of hammer-hardening due to the change of structure in a metal but slightly malleable and susceptible to hammer-hardening.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
COLOURS OF TOPAZ.

Although the normal colour of the topaz is yellow, a blue variety is by no means uncom- mon. The Siberian stones, for example, have a bluish tinge, while those from the High- lands of Scotland are of a sky-blue colour. Blue topazes, again, occur in the granite of the Mourne Mountains in Ireland. The colour of a topaz, in fact, as of other precious stones, de- pends on the presence of some accidental im- purity, and the purest varieties are colourless. These are found in Brazil, and are termed Gouttes d'Eau. When cut in facets they closely resemble diamonds in lustre and br:lliancy. In a similar way the clear crystalline form of corundum (oxide of aluminium) when red is known as the ruby, when blue it is the sap- phire, and when yellow the Oriental topaz. This is distinguished from the true topaz by its greater hardness. Another stone some- times known as topaz is a yellow varietykof quartz. The true topaz is to be distinguished from these by its hardness, which is interme- diate between" that of quartz and corundum. ,and by the fact that it is composed of alumi- nium, silicon, and fluorine. The pink topaz of the jeweller is probably always an artificial product as regards its colour. In 1750, a Parisian jeweller, Dumelle, discovered' that the yellow Brazilian topaz when moderately heated acquired a rose-pink colour. The stone is wrapped in German tin- der, which is then ignited. The resulting ".burnt topaz" is sometimes known as Bra- zilian ruby." So the blue topaz is sometimes known as "Brazilian sapphire.", A pale green topaz is known LIZ aquamarine," a name which rightly belongs to the sea-green beryl.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
OCEAN ABYSSES.

The .greatest ocean depths are not found in the Atlantic, as there are veritable abysses to be met with on the other side of the globe, says the Scientific American. Close to New Zealand the water attains a depth of five and a-half miles in the Kermadec and Tonga ravines, which in themselves attain a height of 29,530 ft., while they are separated from each other by a chain of mountains of 9,850 ft. height. There is also the Aleutian ravine, which reaches a depth of 23,000 ft. The Atlantic Ocean, for instance, covers two vast valleys; one of these passes be- tween the Cape Verde islands and the Azores, and is of great depth. It runs close up .to Europe and comes to an end close to the British Islands, where a ridge or crest of land separates it from the basin of the North Sea. The other valley runs in the main parallel to the first, from which it is separated by an elongated strip of land of which the Azores form a super-marine 'continuation. This strip does not exceed a depth lof 9,8501 t., while its height amounts to 6,560 ft. The first volley, like its confrere, is also very deep. its bottom being situated at. a depth of nearly four miles below the surface. Passing along South America, and leaving the Bermudas to the left, it passes along Newfoundland and Labrador, finally ending just south of Greenland. The sub-Atlantic landscape thus consists of two vast parallel valleys, separated from each other by a range of mountains. Further north the land lies higher and the sea is, relatively speak- ing. shallow. Between Greenland and the Conti- nent, close to Iceland and the Channel Islands, there is a huge plain free from any depressions worthy of mention. It is quite clear that at one time England was connected to the continent.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
RARE EARTH THORIA.

The .most important discovery connected with the mineralogical survey of Ceylon in 1904, says , an official report, was that of the occurrence of thorium-bearing minerals in Ceylon. Of this the most important is thorianite, a mineral new to ' science, and containing 70 to 80 per cent, of the rare earth thoria, which ois used for the manufac- ture of incandescent gas mantles. The Govern• ment was informed that £3O per cwt. was being offered in England for thorianite containing from 70 to 72 per cent, of thoria. With a view to en- couraging further search for this valuable mineral a notice was published giving the above particulars, and also stating the places where thorianite and thorite had been found. Intend- ing prospectors were ,informed that the Govern- ment would for three years undertake to levy no royalty on this mineral, except in the case traction from from Crown lands, where the permissism to wash will be by agreement on liberal terms. There is a large area, including all the Pro- vince of Sabaragamuwa and part of the Central, Western, and Southern Provinces, where the mineral may be looked for. Search is now being made in many localities; it is not possible to say at present how far a regular supply can be an- ticipated. About 1401 b. of thorianite, which were received from Mr. W. D. Holland, who first obtained this mineral, were sent to the Crown agents in November, and were sold by Professor Dunstan. of the Imperial Institute, for 1E95 2s. 4d. Professor Dunstan is taking further steps to obtain reports on the commercial value : of the sample sent to him and for supplying such further information as may lead to more dis- coveries. The Mineralogical Survey were further engaged in examining the gemming districts in Raharsigainnwa and the Southern Province, in investigating discoveries of corundum and w heavy minerals containing rare elements, as, for example, allanite, and aeveral minerals belonging to the Samarskite group.

STOCKTON HEATH.

PARISH COUNCIL AND TRAM FARRS.—The annual meeting of the Parish Council was held at the Hall Cafe on Friday evening. Mr. J. H. Clarke presided at the commencement, end there were present Messrs. F. Pearson, H J. Westbrook, J. Hallows, F. S. Welsby. J. Whitley, F. Warburton, T. R. Westbrook, J. W. Bouchier, J. Fairhurst, and E. Bradshaw (clerk).—On the motion of Mr. H. J. Westbrook, seconded by Mr. F. Pearson, Mr. J. W. Bouchier was re-elected chairman for the ensuing year, and he was also accorded a hearty vote of thanks for his past services.—The Clerk read a letter from the deputy Town Clerk of Warrington res- pecting a recent communication of the Parish Council with a view to obtaining permission for workmen to travel from Stockton Heath to Messrs. Fletcher's works for a halfpenny. The Tramways Committee, after con- sideration, said they could not see theit way to ft:cede to the request. Messrs. Bouchier, H. J. Westbrook, and F. Pearson, were requested to wait upon the Tram- ways Committee with regard to half-penny stages on the Wilderspool route.—Messrs. Fairhurst and J. Whit- ley were appointed overseers, and were thanked for their services.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
HAD VISIBLE MEANS.

Robert Sling, chemical labourer,charged at St. Helens on Monday with lodging out and having no visible means of subsistence, surprised the court by producing a shilling, and stating that he had lodgings, but acci- dently fell asleep while talking to a man.—Police- constable Merrill said he was sent for from the chemical works, and when he locked Sling up and searched him he had only 3A. on him.—Sling said the shilling was in his pocket all the time.—The magistrates asked if Sling had been put in a cell with other prisoners.—The police said he had. They 1 had frequently to turn this man out of the works, and he had been convicted twenty- five times of sleeping out at those werks.—The bench sent Sling to gaol for twenty-eight days.

NEW TALE STARTS TO-DAY;

`A SON OF EMPIRE.'

This is the title of the story which commences in our issue of to-day. It is from the pen of the well-known novelist and journalist, MORLEY ROBERTS,

- A. SON OF EkpIRE,,

BY MORLEY ROBERTS.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
AUTHOR OF

"The Master of the Silver Sea," "The Adventures of a Ship's Doctor,"" Strong Men and True,"" The Keeper of the Waters," " The Colossus," etc.

The Author of our story has a world-wide reputation. He went to Australia before he was twenty years of age, and worked on the Victorian railroads and in the bush of New South Wales, chiefly with sheep and cattle, Then he served as a sailor, and ultimately found his way into Govermnent employment but the work of a civil servant was too slow for him, and he started on a long series of travels. Few men have had such a varied experience as MORLEY ROBERTS.

'A SON OF EMPIRE.'

===== 0002601_19060428_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
A SON OF EMPIRE.'

SOMETHING ABOUT THE STORY:

Madge Gretton, while touring abroad with her mother, becomes acquainted,in an uncon- ventional way,with a certain Captain Richard Blundell, a military man of great reputation as a traveller and linguist,and whose advance- ment in the army is hindered by an old feud with one of his superiors. Madge is an ardent Imperialist. She long ago made up her mind to marry a man such as Blundell. Her mother strives to make her respect the conventions, but one morning the girl secretly goes mountaineering with Blundell, who is interested in her, and she falls inlay() with him in earnest. ghe goes to see him off when he departs for Dum-Dum, and he kisses her. It transpires that his greatest enemy in the service is Sir Daniel Fielding, the Adjutant- General, the father of Madge's closest friend, Annette Fielding. Sir Daniel Fielding's intense hatred of Blundell arose from a light affair of the heart, in which the younger man won and made his chief appear ridiculous. Sir Daniel has had a life-long revenge by preventing Blundell's advancement in the service. When Madge returns home she casts about for a plan whereby she can dis- cover the cause of Sir Daniel's hatred, hoping afterwards to obtain some influence which can be used in Blundell's favour. She finds that the hatred is imp?acable, and then determines at the opening of a frontier war to aid her hero by sending a cipher cable- gram in Sir Daniel's name requesting the Commander-in-Chief in India to provide Blundell with employment in a distinguished position. This is done. Blundell obtains a command and distinguishes himself greatly. In the very moment of success word comes that the cablegram has been repudiated as a forgery, and Blundell is placed in an ugly position, which will end in his being • cashiered unless he can offer an explanation. He knows almost intuitively that it was Madge who concocted the cablegram, but bravely determines to shield her, let the cost be what it may. The scene shifts again to England, and Madge soon knows through the Pieldings that the forgery has been found out, and has the bitterness of knowing that she has probably ruined the man she hoped to assist. While he, unknown to her, is doing all he can to shield her, she is making every sacri- fice to rehabilitate him; and so love's conflict goes on, the man and woman both bent upon acts of self-sacrifice. An admirable and intensely vivid tale is brought to an end in a But our readers must learn what the ending is from the story itself. 4A SON OF EMPIRE.'

!A SON OF EMPIRE.'

The Opening chapters appear in our issue of TH i.S DAY FRIDAY;

The voice of the Old Toryism TRADE UNIONS was heard in the House of AND THE LAW. Commons on Wednesday, and it came curiously enough from a new member, and formed the material of a maiden speech. Mr. CLAVELL SALTER. opposed the Trades Disputes Bill because, in his opinion, it will facilitate strikes, legalise boy- cotting, picketing, and intimidation, lead to wholesale breaches of contract, and give a general license to persecution immediately on the occur- rence of a trade dispute. Much the same set of ideas must bays been in the mind of the late Lord SALISBURY when he spoke of Trade Unions as "these cruel organizations." But to find them animating politicians in the present day is unexpected and surprising. Mr. SALTER, no doubt believes what he said. He has convinced himself that the Bill for which the Government is res- ponsible will disastrously affect the daily life and labour of many thousands of working people. It is a Bill, he says, which proposes to give Trade Unions the right to do wrong with impunity. Does anybody believe that if this were true thk House of Commons would he So indifferent to - sequences that it would permit the measure to go through its second reading without a division? The truth is that there is an almost general recog- nition of the fact that the Bill merely proposes to restore a condition of things which actually existed up to the time of the Taff Vale decision. Nobody is really afraid of Trade Unions nowadays. It has been found that, so far from promoting strikes, they prevent them. Where shall we to-day find the enlightened employer who would not prefer the easy and intelligible means of settling dis- putes which is provided by the Trade Unions to the methods which it was formerly necessary to employ? Mr. SALTER and young Mr. Bowris, who led the Opposition, appear to think that the Trade Unionists wish to behave after the manner of the Broadhead days. They have not taken the trouble to ascertain, as they easily might have done, that it is the Trade Unions 'lather than the law which have put an end to out- rages in industrial disputes.

In the course of the able and lucid speech in which he moved the second reading,the SOLICITOR- GENERAL referred to his experience of the great engineers' strike in the North of England a few years since—an event which, by the way, was rather a lock-out than a strike, and therefore all the more likely to create bitter and violent feeling amongst those who suffered by it. It lasted six months, s.iid Sir WILLIAM ROBION ; over 20,000 men were concerned in it in and around Newcastle alone, and yet, in all that period, only one case, and that a doubtful one, was taken before the Bench in connection with the struggle. The Solicitor-General might save drawn another striking illustration of the moderation of Trade Unions. In the same district picketing, or peaceful persuasion, was never more axtensively employed than during the miner's strike. Succes- sive batches of men were taken to Newcastle to fill the places of the men on strike. SJine were , from distant placesan our own country; many were \ brought over from Germany. In general, they were ignorant of the existence of any strike, and when they were told of the real state of things they were usually eager to go back home, especially as the strike organisation Tprovided the funds enabled them to do so. A quite peaceful industrial revo- lution was carried out on that occasion without any such acts as those the imagination of which has terrified the soul of Mr. Gravara, SALTER.

How differently the same thing may he described according to the point of view may be well seen by a comparison. of Mr. SALTER'S frantic words with the calm and simple statement of the SOLICITOR- GENRRAL. The Bill, said Sir WILLIAM ROBSON, aims at the removal of exceptional disabilities which are deemed to be contrary to the general spirit of our taws. That is the case for this measure in a nutshell. The remaining stages will probably present almost as little difficulty as did the second reading stage on Wednesday. The Government has met the Labour members by a proposal to settle in Committee the one matter over which there has bean disagreement. It will be the business of the lawyers in the Government to construct a clause which other lawyers cannot creep through.

Earthquakes and volcanic outbursts have been so fre- quent of late thatithe world is becoming a very alarm- ing place to live in. For destruction of life and property the earthquake at San Francisco is probably:without parallel, unless the earthquake of Lisbon be the one superior calamity of the same kind. The loss of life at Lisbon was caleulated at 50,000. Half that number of lives was lost in Peru nearly a century later from the same cause. There have been numerous earthquakes which have involved a sacrifice of

20,000 lives at least. They have occurred at places Widely separated by distance. There was one such destructive earthquake at Algiers in 1716. More than half a century had passed away before the occurrence of the Lisbon catastrophe, and nearly 200 years before the memorable nnth ors'. at St: Pierre. Nothing that we know enables us to connect these calami- ties one with another. They equally defy fore- 'knowledge and explanation. It cannot even be said with any feeling of real conviction that, a trict'which has once been attacked is likely to suffer again. That would he true of some places and not of others. There was no reason to think that -Lisbon was not as firmly established as the base of Mont Blanc, yet in 1755 it suddenly became yawning chasm, in which whole streets and all their inhabitants were swallowed up. An event so extensively calami tons might reasonably have seemed to forbid the building of another city on the same site ; yet it did net, nor has it deterred the builder of &ties in any other instance. Men always go hack to the site that has been devastated. Sin Francisco, instead of being densely populated, would have been a wilderness, oc!easionally swept by the high tides of the sea, if former earthquakes had hone seriously regarded as a warning. It is bnilt on what had long been acknowledged to he a weak part of the earth's crust. The earlier houses, as in the case of Japan, were for that reason built mainly of wood. How- ever, as somebody wrote a few years since, "the builders are no longer hampered by the fear of earth- quakes." The terrihie liability to disaster had been forgotten. San Francisco is the city of which the Amee-ican boasts even more than of New York or Chicago. Its name is mentioned through- out the world, it is probable, more frequently; than that of any ether place in the United Sates vet everyhody seemed to have forgotten that the houses were formerly huilt of wood because there Was danger of earthquakes. The troth is that the chances are sornethino like 1,000 to 1 against an earthquake strilring'any large centre of population.l It is much more likely to lose itself in desert apanes. In England there are two' distinguished tebsey,ars of seismic nhenemena—Dr. Mrr,o,TE and -nr.l4vrsoo4r. Their investigations are carried on indenemiently of each other, hot they both came to the same conclusion, that 1905 was a great earthquake year. Nethertheless, it is not in that charaeter that the year will he memorable. The big earthquakes which might have destroyed great cities took place in Central Asia,ameng tribes who never build houses and wh I never write history. Wo should have known nothing about them hut Tor the existence of instruments which record earth tremors the whole world over. But how terrible is the difference made by efew hundreds or a few scores of miles! The Stn !Francisco earthquake 'night have lost itself in the sela or in the Sierras, and have never been heard of. It just happens to Strike a part of the earth on which great crowds c'f human kind have congregated, and there is a catastrophe at which the world stands aghast. The lesson that is driven home by such events as this i 3 that of human helplessness in the face of an 'Piloted assertion of natural forces. What has Inened could neither be foreseen nor prevented. 'hing quite like it may occur again whilst the lasts ; and 'yet there is po ground for feel. 114 confident that it will not repeat itself in the text half-score of days.

Serious attention will have THE DUSTPROBLEM. to be given to the dust nuisance along the country roads. The rapid increase in motor traffic is mak- ing some of the best highways almost unendurable for the pedestrian, cyclist, and humble carter. Not only are these legitimate users of the road entitled to complain loudly but the farmers are finding that the dust is causing actual damage as well as annoyance. The herbage of the fields bordering on main roads is becoming 90 impregnated with dust as to be unfit for grazing; one case is cited of eight ewes having died as the result of eating the gritty grais. Such fruits as strawberries and rasp- berries are in danger of being rendered quite unmarketable, and altogether the dust cloud of the motorist has become something which has to be considered in anything but a spirit of levity. The blame cannot be laid entirely at the door of the motorists. They bay° a perfect right to drive their cars om the highway, and they cannot help raising the dust if it is there. The rerrpely seems to be in road-making on lines more in keeping with the class of traffic which may be expected for the next generation. The ordinary macadam, sand and grit 'surfaces will not do for fast traffic because of their dust-producing properties. Some heavier material after the nature of tarred macadam or asphalt will, of urgent necessity, have to be introduced intoi road-making. The extra cost involved need not fall on the rural authorities. A special tax on Imotors would provide a sum large enough to re-model the road-surfaces ; or the fines so sedulously collected by the police might be devoted to the same purpose with appreciable effect. Another suggestion we have heard is that the inland revenue authorities should be made to dis- gorge that part of the income tax which might be computed as accruing from the individual expenditure on motoring of each motor-owner who was an income-tax payer. Thus, if a man with, say, £l,OOO a year, spent £2OO per year on motoring, and the income tax was is. in the pound, £lO of his income-tax should go to road-maintenance instead of to Inland Revenue. This is, we think, an original notion and is by no means impracticable. At any rate, whatever may be the ultimate solution of the dust problem, the question of 2 s. d. will largely enter into it, and this is one way of "raising the wind "to lay the dust which neither road-makers nor motorists will object to.

There is talk of another

ONCE AGAIN, expedition to " the South Tun SOUTH POLE. Pole." Captain Scott's voy- age brought us a good deal of information. Various facts of geography and geology were learnt. But it is urged that these investigations, to secure the best results, should he early resumed, and that some continuity of policy should mark the explorations which claina the merit of scientific direction. Sir Clements Mark• ham laments that the good ship Discovery was not acquired by the Admiralty with the view to further Antarctic exploring. It was necessary to sell her some months age to raise funds for the publication of the results of the expedition: She crossed the Antarctic circle in January of 1902, and was away from this country thre 3 years, the greater part of that time imprisoned in the ice field. Her party reached a point further South than any previous explorers,Latitude 82deg 17min. ; and the important fact was ascertained that the ice-barrier floated, and was not attached to the land. Bat there is still part of the moun- tain range extending to the south to discover; there are problems to decide as to the character of King Edward VII. Land, the southern extent of the great ice-barrier, and the distribution of land and water on the other side of the Pole. This quest has tempted Lieutenant Michael Baine. The Lieutenant was in the last expedition, and nothing daunted, is asking for funds to fit out another. Practical people who can find plenty to do at home will ask sometimes whether these searches in the abysmal darkness are really worth the candle.

There are many people who BLACK LOYALTY. Seem to think that to talk of a black man being loyal to a white race is abstird. Such people have not lived amongst natives. There can be no servants more loyal to their masters and mistresses than black servants. The love of a black woman for a white infant, awl that infant's love for its nurse, are extraordinary. Likewise,the nigger who accom- panies his master through the jungle has a profound veneration for the superior knowledge which the European possesses. Loyalty, in its literal sense, of course, means allegiance to the letter of the law; but the man who is most loyal to his country or a district in which he resides is actuated by nobler motives than the mere fulfilment of some legal stipulation. To be really loyal to a government it is necessary to appreciate the good which that government is able to do, intends to do, or has done. But the first trust of manhood is the duty of being, in the best sense, loyal to himself. This is not always an easy matter. So many con- siderations confuse the mind sometimes as to what constitutes real or ideal duty that conduct frequently owes its direction to impulse rather than logic. When loyalty consists in merely bow- ing the knee to what is called earthly greatness the world is better without it. What is wanted is rather that loyalty which, after everything has been done to avert trouble, when the trouble COMPS, instead of screaming, "I told you so," and complaining that "everythink goes contrairy," puts the best face on the matter, although the oyalist is a " lorn creetur, like Mrs. Gurnrnidge.

Some time ago Mr. Gilbert THE DUEL, made us acquainted with the ingenious variation of the "code of the dtpllo," which offered the advantage of never in reality depriving anyone of his life; The thing was sinaiple. Instead of fight- mg with falchions bright in the old way, the disputants each drew a card from a pack, and " he who draws the lowest shall, so 'tie said, he hence- forth dead; in fact, a legal ghost"! Nothing could be more excellent, though Ludwig in this particular case found that assuming the dead man's responsibilities brought certain embarrassments which he had not contemplated. Two young men in Warsaw acted somewhat. on the idea the other day. They both fell in love with the same lady, and, instead of drawing their swords and fighting for her on the spot, they went to the ante-room and played a game of cards. The one who lost, however, did not carry out the Gilbertian idea. He was not content to be a legal ghost. so he immediately drew a revolver and converted himself into a very real one. His companion, seeing what had happened, did not even stn nto see the lady he had won, hut made tracts for Berlin, apparently to be out of roach of the law. One is only sorry that the excellence of the Gilhertian idea should have been so ignored by the combatants. If the only result of using cards is that the lover is to shoot himself, it is bettor to return to the &mil() of France, where real weapons are used on the strict understanding that neither combatant is to hurt the other.

The Examiner, Saturday, April 28 1906.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

, Our new serial "A. Son of Empire," by Morley Roberts commences in this issue of the " Examiner.'"

* * * Some of the holes in the wood-pavement of Sankey- street, would be less dangerous if they were fenced round. * * *

Has Lady Warwick really sold a "drawer-full "of jewels to help the Socialist cause? How kind and sweet of her !

* Nothing irritates fox hunters more than for an old cow to get in front of the hounds and rush madly ahead with her tail in the air and terror in her soul. It makes it appear as though they were hunting the cow. . .

* * The death of the King's oldest subject is announced. George Fletcher, of Pomeroy, county Tyrone, haying passed away at the reputed age of 118 years. As the oldest British subject he was some time ago presented hy the King with a gold snuffbox. * * *

The scholars in the upper standards at Fairfield schools have gone " back to the land." On certain days they may be, seen busy with garden tools tilling the ground in the vicinity of Fairfield Hall. It is said they are becoming quite ardent agriculturalists. * *

Mr. J.ltamsay MacDonotld, M.P., the new chairman of the 1.L.P., is a man of fine qualities. He was the son of farm labourers and has risen by dint' of hard work and ideals. A capable and efficient organiser, a tactician, and an orator of no mean powers he still finds time to write books.

* * * The Electricity Department has contracted to light the town clock with electricity for the sum of :£2O per annum. We hope they will make a better job of it than the Gas Committee have done. Trinity Clock has long been the worst lighted pubic time-keeper of any importance that we are familiar with. *** The Countess of Warwick made an illuminating reference to religious instruction at a Labour meeting in Stockport on Monday. She described the con- troversy as a curse, and added : " Let the children grow up and think for themselves as to what sect or religious body they desired to be attached and give them time `to learn.'" * * *.

Where an anti-vaccinationist has taken the trouble to lose a day's work and come before his local law court to request exemption in conformity with the Act of 1808, then, foolish and mistaken though he is, the magistrates who refuse his application are themselves utterly wrong, and are, indeed, among the very worst enemies of vaccination.—" British Medical Journal." * * * _ _

Speaking at Manchester on Monday, Mr. W. Runciman, M.P., alluding to the education controversy, said :--" What struck the plain man most was the way in which Christian teachers appeared to bate one another's religion—how frightened they were of their own truths being taught by someone not of their own communion, and what a terror there appeared to be of the Bible." * * *I. There was some little confusion at the Education Committee's meeting on Monday in regard to a certain vote. Councillor Craik moved an amendment and then surprised himself and everybody else by being the solitary member to vote against it. The worthy Councillor was under the impression that the negative which he so loudly proclaimed was driected against another motion.

* * It is in some way gratifying to find that, while the " Palace of Justice" is not regarded with a very favourable eye locally, the Home Office—which did not pay the bill—holds it up as an object of admiration. On Wednesday, a deputation from Sunderland visited Warrington at the suggestion of the Home Office and was very much impressed with the glories of the local Police headquarters, and the excellence of the organ- isation and methods in vogue. * * *

The enormous fortunes o-f to-day, both individnal and corporate, must he grappled with. It was his personal conviction that it would ultimately be neces- sary to consider the adoption of a national scheme of a progressive tax on the transfer of fortunes, swollen beyond healthy limits, and to prevent any individual from inheriting over a certain amount. There should be a system of national supervision of inter-State Corporations to cope with the evils of over-capitalisa- tion.—President Roosevelt.

* * * Mr. Justice Bargrave Deane has been complaining of the " thinness" of many of the divorce cases which come before him. Tie desires to issue a public warning that in cases of a wife petitioning on the ground of cruelty and misconduct, one act of cruelty only will not do. Two charges of cruelty, at least, must be proved. ThiA suggests that like the dog and his first bite, the wrathful husband is entitled to his first smack. The aggrieved wife will surely feel that this is insult added to injury.

; * Warrington does not stand 'alone in regard to its lively Council meetings. There was a remarkable scene at Wednesday's meeting of the Wigan Council. In the course of an altercation Mr. Fletcher (a Labour member) applied the terms " coward " and " cad " to Alderman Gee. The latter said he would ram the words down the speaker's throat but for " physical disability." The Mayor left the chair on a withdrawal being refused, and the meeting was broken up in con- fusion. We learn that Alderman Gee has since tendered his resignation from the Council as a protest.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
* * *

An application has been made at Leighton Buzzard for a licence to sell intoxicating liquors at a three days' bazaar in aid of the Parish Church new organ fund. An organ thus procured with the aid of the Church and the Devil should make an interesting adjunct to religious services. The superintendent of the police explained that the vicar would not be able to dispose of all the gifts he had received unless the licence were granted. This " explanation " makes matters worse, and it is no w9nder the magistrates adjourned the application for the personal attendance of the Vicar. • .

** * ,

The annual returns for the Wesleyan Methodist Church have just been collected by the " Methodist Recorder." The Connexional total is 498,285 mem- bers, or 13,406 more than a year ago. It is exactly thirty years since so large an increase was last reported, the net gain of 1876 being 14,876. This year's increase is 2,680 more than last year's. There are 38,192 persons on trial, a decrease of 8,079 but still a much larger number than two years ago, and above the average. The junior members for the first time exceed one hundred thousand, the exact figures being 102,753. There are upon the church rolls in all grades of membership in this country 639,230 persons. Finally the unprecedented number of candi- dates for the ministry last year is surpassed this year. There are in all 234. * * *

! 'Peaceful Lymm was worked into a state of excite- ment on 'Tuesday by the escapades of the local fire engine. An outbreak of fire had occurred at Lvmm Hall and the raising of the alarm was the signal for the opening of a chapter of accidents. First the rope attached to the firo bell snapped and when at last ' everything had been got in readiness. it was discovered that there were no horses available for the engine. A passing steed was pressed into service but as the engine needs two horses the arrangement was some- what lop-sided and the progress of the engine was uncertain. An organ grinder was regarding the pro- &edings with interest when the engine upset his peace of mind and incidentally his organ also. A cage containing fancy birds was smashed and the captives flew away rejoicing. When at length Lymm Hall was reached the fire was cut ! * * *

The unusual spectacle of -a Councillor doing public penance for intemperance was witnessed at Birkenhead on Sunday afternoon. A crowd of about 1,000 people assembled at an. open air meeting in response to an advertisement in the following terms :—Subject What intoxicants have done for me. A. personal testimony. Speaker : .Councillcir T. Major Thompson. The Councillor, who is the leader of the local Protestant movement, confessed that a fortnight ago he. bad been fined at Liverpool for intemperance and as the case had not been reported in the Press he took this opportunity of publicly acknowledging Ins offence. lie bad tendered his resignation from the Prdiestant party, but it had been declined. " Hence- forth "be concluded " I am on the side of total abstinence, and I shrill tell my story often as a.wap- ing to others. This has been the means of bringing about a great change in my life." After bearing this statement the meeting passed a vote of continued con- fidence in Councillor Thompson.
