I The congregation of St. Michael's Church, Runcorn, have suffered a great loss by the resignation of their choirmaster, Mr. Charles Pownall. At one time Mr. Pownall was both organist and choirmaster, and q relin uished the former position to devote his wholeat- I tention to the duties of the latter.He has also interested himself in the work of the Sunday school, and for many years was superintendent. The period of a work so eminently successful is a fine record. A suitable testi- monial is to be presented to Mr. Pownall, and subscrip- tions have been freely and generously given. ST HELENS.

TO FIGHT CO-OPERATORS. ST. HELENS TRADERS TO RE-OPEN THE WAR

- WITH £50,000 FUND. The excitement aroused in trading circles in the North of England by the action of the St. Helens tradesmen in declaring war against the local co-opera- tive society some eighteen months ago—a movement which rapidly spread in other towns up and down the country—will shortly he intensified by new operations, and the promulgation of a new scheme on vigorous lines by the energetic Private Traders' Defence Association of St. Helens of which Alderman J. Green, J.P., pre— sident, and Mr. J. Cottarn, hon. sec., are the moving spirits.—The St. Helens and District Traders' Defence Association have met "to consider a scheme for the better organisation of the traders of the district " in a movement which will benefit trade and the town gener- ally."—The report states that" the scheme proposes to distribute £so,ooo—which sum will he fully secured and will probably shortly be doubled—amongst the trading public, and to give them advantages and benefits which co-operative societies cannot give." FATAL JUMP FROM A CAR.—LANIPLIGHTER'S DEATH.

The inquest on William Thomas 'Morley, aged thirty- six, a lamplighter, of Warrington New-road, was held at ; Peasley-cross, St. Helens, on Saturday. by Mr. Brig- house. Morley's father, an Army pensioner, said the deceased was born at Singapore thirty-six years ago, and when he was nine years of age he lost his right arm. He had been a lamplighter for the St. Helens Corporation for the last ten years. John Matthews, conductor of one of the St. Helens electric tramcars, said that on Monday last deceased got on the car at Windle City, and he placed his lamp- lighting stick on the rear platform of the car. When they passed through the loop near the Park Hotel the stick slipped off the car. The deceased jumped up from. his seat to catch it, and the signal was rung for the driver to stop, but before the car stopped Morley jumped off with his face in the opposite direction to that in which the car was going. He foil flat on his back, and was 'picked up unconscious. Deceased was taken home, and afterwards to the St. Helens Hospitah but he never regained consciousness, and died early on Thursday morning. A verdict of "Accidental death was returned, he blame being attached to the conductor of the car.

Left alone with a baby in a cot, two Canning Town children stuffed her with bread and milk, and the poor infant died from suffocation.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

ASEITON COLLIERY FATALITY.

On Monday morning Mr. S. Brighouse resumed the inquest at the Bryn Working Men's Club, concerning the death of Henry Patterson, 38, collier, of 48, Whithill-street, Ashton, who was killed on April 11th by a fall of roof in the Wigan six feet nine, number seven pit of the Garswood Hall Collieries. The inquest was adjourned from April 12th, in order that Mr. Flail, his Majesty's Inspectur of Mines, might inspect the scone of the fall.—Mr. Hall now informed the jury that he had made an inspection of the place. Nothing had been removed, and from the fact that so many props were to be seen, it was evident that the management had considered the place sufficiently timbered. Events had unfortunraely proved it otherwise, and now an under- taking had been given to set timber closer together. He had no complaint to make.—The jury thereupon returned a verdict of " Accidental death," no further , evidence being called.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
LORDS AND GOVERNMENT. Lord Newton on the Future.

Lord Newton presided on Saturday at the twentieth annual meeting of the Newton Conservative Association in the Town Hall at Newton-le-Willows. Colonel R. Pilkington, who for seven years represented the 'division in Parliament and was defeated at the last general election, was also present. Lord Newton was Ire-elected president and the other ()dicers were appointed. According to the annual report, the work in the registration courts at the revision of the voters' lists last September showed there was a Unionist gain of 172,the largest since 1897. At the same time the report deplored the defeat of the party in the division, and attributed this largely to misrepresentations, mis- statements, and gross exaggerations made by the Liberal and Labour party to obtain power,and especially with regard to the question of alleged slavery in con- nection with the Chinese labour in South Africa." On the motion of Mr. Fulton Smith, a resolution was adopted protesting against the provisions of the Educa- tion Bill introduced by the present Government. By another resolution a vote of thanks was passed to Colonel Pilkington for his past services as representa- tive of the division. Colonel Pilkington, in thanking all his supporters, denounced the "misrepresentation" which their oppon- ents, made at the last election, but for which, be said, he would still be the member for the Newton division. The Chinese were still in South Africa, and they were going to be there. Colonel Pilkington held that the late Government was one of the best of Governments. Their opponents spoke of the late Government's sxtravagance but he claimed they had made no change worth mentioning "Wherever you look," he said," the charges they made are vanishing into thin air, the promises made cannot he kept, and they are simply doing, when they do well, the the things we dsd before." As for the Education Bill, it was going to reverse all the work which Liberal and Conservative Governments had done in the vast to build up education—and religious education—in the schools. Colonel Pilkington protested against the I desertion of Lord Milner, who after his services to the Empire ought to be crowned with laurel and not covered with dirt. (Applause).

===== 0002601_19060428_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
A POLITiCAL MySTERY.

Lord Newton, replying to a vote of thanks, declared that he found some consolation in the fact that the disaster which overtook the party in that diviiion was slight compared with that in other constituencies. Their candidate, too, was an honest man. (Hear, hear.) "It will be a mystery to many people," he said, "why this constituency should prefer to be represented by its present member rather than our late member, and I have no doubt many ,people are wondering why they voted in the way they did at the last election. The House of Commons, however, is now anything but an agreeable place of resort. Largely composed of Pro- Boers and passive resisters, it is not surprising it should bean uncongenial place of assembly for all right-minded persons. Further than that. lain told that speaking in the House of Commons at the present moment is like addressing a very rough meeting, crowded by your opponents, in a Radical neighbourhood, that no sort of respect is paid to age, experience, or ability, and that the vast majority that follows that eminent statesman Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman—nominally—is chiefly used in silencing the minority. It will be a standing wonder to a future generation that such a House should be returned."

===== 0002601_19060428_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
-TIIE FUNCTIONS OF THE LORDS.

Lord Newton further expressed the opinion that "the largest Liberal majority on record " was led " by the weakest Prime. Minister of modern times." On the sub- ject of the Trade Disputes Bill he said that to place trade organisations entirely above the law was clearly an unjustifiable proceeding. If the Bill passed the House of Commons and came to the House of Lords, that House would be entitled most clearly to restore the bill to the original condition in which it was intro- duced by Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman's Government and probably in the long run the Prime Nlinister would' be bound to admit that the House of Lords on the whole had taken a correct view of the case. As to the Education Bill,Lord Newton declared it was not an Education Bill at all. It was really a religious jerrymandering bill and its peculiarity was that it did notaatisfy anybody except the political Nonconformists and that class of opin!on which was represented by the passive resisters. It appeared to be a sort of electioneering debt which was due to those gentlemen for their efforts on behalf of the Liberal cause at the last election. The Bill repre- sented the triumph of the passive resister, and was far from ending this perpetually recurring controversy. If this Bill became law it would be necessary at a future time to repeal it and to restore something approaching the present state of things. Lord Newton eulogised Lord Milner as one of the greatest of English- men. En concluding he denied that he had any fanatical admiration for the House of Lords. He bad as keen a sense of its failings as any Radical but he was certain it would not do anything foolish. It was not in the least likely to fly in the face of public opinion and act contrary to the wish of the people. The chief value of it was that it was a revising Chamber, and if measures came up which clearly bore the mark of injustice or the brute force of a temporary majority —however big—then he did not see why this power of revision should not be applied ; and if applied judici- ously and impartially, he believed it would not be resented by the country, but would meet with the approval of all who bad at heart the interests of the United Kingdom. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19060428_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
BEAUTIFUL WARRINGTON SOCIETY.

Shady Nooks and Corners.

Many people have a mistaken idea that unless the genial rays of sunshine are able to get into every nook and corner of their garden or yard, it is of little use ; trying to grow anything, and give up the idea of a garden. One often hears the remark that " our garden I is facing due north, and we know nothing will grow." ; Now there are gardens of shade and gardens of light; , the two combined form pleasing contrasts to each other ; when properly cultivated. I In the garden of light or sunshine are all the glorious Iwealth of dainty and gorgeous blossom, as well as the , ; richness of the hues of the foliage. Sunshine brings out the wealth of colour in all its brightness. In the garden of shadow we see less of the bright colours, and snore of variety of form with its cool, refreshing , greenery, with enough modest colour to impart a sense lof restfulness. Who has not seen the little stream runn- ing through the shady dell with ferns growing on each side without feeling a sense of peacefulness? Now it ; is my contention that it is just as passible to have the ; shady portions of a garden as interesting as sunny ones. The great cause of failure in the creation and manage- ment of ti shady garden is that people attempt to de ; irnpossibfe things. For instance, they aim at making a shady nook as bright in colouring as is possible in sunny situations. It is a well-known fact that flower- ing plants, as a rule, require a large amount of sunshine !to devplop their blossoms to perfection. Plant them in sunless positions and they only produce foliage, Now there are flowers, and a still larger number of , other plants, that delight in shade, and which would, if ' properly selected and planted, prove a success, and be in every way as interesting and beautiful as those ; planted under the genial influence of sunshine. ' In many of the shady dells we find creeping Jenny, ivy, St. John's wort, harebells, and mosses covering the sloping banks ; and primroses, bluebells, foxgloves, I and ferns, bedecking the surface of the bank and copes I with alwealth of beauty. Imitate those as near as you can. The plants for such nooks and corners may con- sist of several sorts of lilies—Hansen's lily, the ; Martagon lily, Japanese lily, sivhite and red, jay lily, , monk's hood, larkspurs. The foregoing vary in height I from three to four feet. Those averaging one to three feet are columbines, German irises, iu variety, Japanese anemones, Campanula persieifolia and macrantha and hardy ferns. Of smaller plants, the London pride, creep- ing Jenny, funkias,coloured primroses, whiter, )1. cress, saxilraga, hypnoides and Campanula carpatica. The following bulbs will also do well—Scilla campanulata, white rose, and blue, snowdrops, crocuses, American cowslip, and daffodils. Before planting, the border should be properly pre- pared by deep digging and liberal manuring. The soil ; ought to be dug at least two feet deep and a good layer of manure placed at the bottom, also one a foot below I the surface. If heavy, add road sweepings, loaf soil, I and vegetable refuse, also some quick-lime. If light, add clay or heavy loam and cow or pig manure—not , horse manure. Plant the bulbs in October, or November, and the others in spring or autumn. In April, lightly fork over the surface, but on no account dig deeply. ; Every third or fourth year lift the whole of the plants, ' dig deeply, and manure again as before. Then you will I got healthy growth and an abundance of flowers. J. F.

Crosfield are at present in Rome attending the sixth Congress of Applied Chemistry, which opened on Wednesday evening with a social re union and commenced business on Thursday morning and continue until May 3rd. The last Congress was held in 1903 in Berlin and it is expected that at the close of the present conference the English delegates will invite the Congress to meet in London on the next occasion.,

Railway Smash .at Widnes. serious accident occurred at a level crossing on the railway in Ann-street East, Widnes, on Thursday evening at 5 o'clock. A. light engine had just passed through the gates towards the sheds when the driver noticed another engine approaching from the sheds on the same line. He at once reversed' the engine and jumped off the footplate. The locomotive consequently returned at a good speed, crashing through the gates at the crossing,and finally splintering a brake van on a St. Helens goods train. Two boys playing near the level crossing were seriously injured by the flying fragments of the gates. Thefts from Warrington Works. 7

ht the Warrington Police Court on Thursday, two youths, named William Lee and George Ellis, hailing from the Bank Quay neighbourhood, pleaded guilty to stealing a quantiy of zinc, value IN., from the White- cross Works. Superintendent Monaghan stated that prisoners were employed at the works. On Wednes- day night they , were seen by Detective Clough, in Golborne-street; carrying something bulky wrapped in sacking. The officer followed them, and prisoners, noticing they were being shadowed, dropped the bag and commenced to run. Lee was caught, and admitted stealing the zinc, and Ellis was afterwards arrested. Ellis, who had been previously convicted for theft,was sent to gaol for three months, and Lee was tined 20s. or one month.

; Warrington Police Entertained. On Wednesday and Thursday evenings the members of the Warrington County Borough police force were entertained to dinner by the Mayor (Alderman Bolton). Oi each occasion half of the force sat down to an ' excellent repast served by Mr. and Mrs. Powis. The usual loyal toasts received suitable recognition and the Mayor's health was drholt with great heartiness. The other toasts included that of the Police Force. The evenings were mainly spent in conviviality and members of the force p-oved themselves excellent entertainers. On Wednesday night the vocalists were P.S. Evans, P.C.'s Hewitt, Holland, Stewart, Hamblett and Newer. A , feature of the pro- ceedings WAS a song "The Warrington County Borough Police," written by the Chief Constable and set to music by Mr. W. H.' Payton, Mus. Ban., and which was sung by P. S. Evans, seven selected members of the force joining in the chorus. On Thursday evening the song was given by P.C. Wood- land, and in addition P.S. Barlow and P.C.'s Unskett, Monks, and Houghton, contributed to the proceedings. On this occasion a number of selections were given by Mr. Lyndon Wyatt who is a guest of the Chief C ci- stable's and who is an unusually capable enter- tainer.

Deputation to Warrington Pollee Establish- ment. On Wednesday a deputation from the Sunderland Watch Committee, with their Chairman and the Chief Constable of that County Borough, visited the Warrington police headquarters where they were received by the Mayor of Warrington and the Chief of Police (Mr. L. Talbot): The police establish- ment of Sunderland is in process of re-organization ! and the deputation came at the suggestion of the Home Office authorities with the object of studying ' the police organization and methods in vogue in this borough. They were much impressed with the details submitted to them and copious notes were taken, together with samples of the various 'local forms and official documents. The Detective department claimed their special attention, with its up-to-date system and ' appliances for the detection of crime and the keeping of criminal records. An exhibition turn-out of the Horse Ambulance, executed in a few seconds, was warmly appreciated. The barrack system for single constables and the accommodation for the residents were also features of special interest. The deputation were struck with the methodical ' division of the voluminous work of the force into separate depart- ments, each under a responsible head. The system of licensing street-trading children was also minutely observed. At the close of their inspection the visitors were emphatic in their expressions of admiration, and many of the more modern features which characterize the police system in Warrington will doubtless be I adapted in Sunderland.

The Prosecution of a Councillor. The summons issued against a Warrington Town Councillor under the Corrupt Practices Acts is trade returnable for Thursday next. The preliminary hearing will therefore open at 10.30 in the morning of that day at the Warrington Police Court. If the magis- strates find that there is a prima fade case they will commit the defendant to take his trial at the Liver- pool Quarter Sessions. Our issue of Friday next will contain a full report of the hearing at Warrington. Labour Bureau. There have been registered during the week 53 persons, making the total number now registered 388. During the week 21 men have obtained work. There are now on the register 191. Any information respect- ing the men required will he willingly given on ring- ing up telephone number 283 any morning from 8 to 12 and afternoon 2 to 3 and it is hoped that employers will avail themsolves of the splendid opportunity of obtaining the best class of workmen with least possible trouble. There are several women on the list who would be glad to get work such as spring cleaning etc. The thanks of the committee are due to the various employers who have, during the past week, made application at the Bureau for work- • men. The foreman who has charge of the erection of the new Post Office, is deing all he can to assist the efforts of the Bureau by obtaining workmen through its agency. School Children and the Baths. The bathing season has commenced and already the school children are showing their appreciat n of ' the privilege, inaugurated last year, by which they are, on Tuesdays, Wednesdays and Fridays, allowed to use the second-class plunge bath free of charge. The arrangement has much to recommend it, and the / chief thing is that the children during the summer months freely avail themselves of the opportunity of bathing. The first batches of boys were admitted on Tuesday night and the laughing, splashing detach- ments numbered 182. On Wednesday evening their ranks had swelled to 214 and the warmer weather will, it is expected, produce an average attendance of 400 a night the average for last year. The question is being asked why should not the privilege be extended to school girls ?

PROGRESS OF RECHABITISM.

An Interesting Local Handbook.

A handbook and diary of an exceptionally interesting character is being issued by the Warrington Consolidated District No. 4 of the Independent Order of Rechabites. The movement has made great progress in Warrington and neighbourhood of late years and the handbook furnishes interesting details of the working of the Order as well as convincing evidence in favour of total absti- nence. The Warrington District includes ‘Varrington, Ashton-in-Makerfield, Bryan, Orrell, Golborne, Low- ton, Earlestown, Pemberton, Garswood, Haydock, ' and Penketh. It comprises 19 adult and 17 juvenile tents, with a membership of 1208 adults, 990 juveniles,399 members' wives and widows,and 8 honorary members. The funds of the district amount to .E 4,832. The handbook, which has been printed at the "Examiner "Office, is make up of 77 pages, and con- tains particulars of all the tents in the district, the scales of contributions and benefits, and the addresses of tent secretaries. A number of strong arguments for total abstinence are given. It is statec4 that the abstainer lives longer, has less illness, is less dependent, is' more self-reliant, and has fewer vices, and that his abstinence from crinu! is most remarkable. Facts from the report of the Committee onPhysical Degeneration are submitted, one of the conclusions arrived at by the Com- mittee being that the abuse of alcoholic stimulants is a most potent and deadly agent of physical degeneration. Other well-known authorities on the subject are also quoted, including Sir Frederick Treves, surgeon to His Majesty the King, who states that alcohol is an insidious poison,that as a work producer it is exceedingly extravagant, and, like other extravagant measures it is about to lead to a physical bankruptcy. A young man, he points out, cannot be "fit" if he takes alcohol. Figures are given showing the financial soundness of the order and the great sums distributed in benefits. The circulation of the handbook should ceztainly do a good service in making better known the objects and advantages of the Omer and enlisting more members for the Warrington district.

The Founder of Mormonism. Was Joseph Smith an imposter?

- - ---- - On Wednesday evening the subject of the debate in Cairo Street Schools was" Was Joseph Smith, the founder of Mormonism, an impostor ?" There was good attendance, the Rev. J. F. Matthews presiding. The CHAIRMAN said the Rev. C. Harvey Cook wished him to say that he had handed to him on Tuesday night a handbill in which a secularist was charged with being an infidel. Mr. Cook instructed him to say that he did not believe a secularist was an infidel and he (Mr. Matthews) of course shared his view in that respect. The discussion of motive, Mr. Matthews pointed out, was a very delicate task, for he fancied that even such colossal frauds as Dr. Docile and men of his stamp not merely believed in themselves whole-heartedly but believed that they alone were right. Time was when to discuss a subject of that kind would have been absolutely impossible and he welcomed such a gathering because it maintained their right of free speech on any topic, at any time, and in any place. (Applause.) Mr. WARD opened the debate in the affirmative. It Mien appeared to him that one of the most damning facts agaipst the assertion that Christiauity was a divine revelation was the huge number of sects existing in Christendom to-day. They had that night to deal with one of the three hundred sects—that commonly known AR Mormonism or the Church of Jesus Christ of the Latter Day Saints which had adherents to the number of about :300,000 with headquarters at Utah, Salt Lake City. The founder of the Church was I a gentleman whom he regarded as a religious I imposter, Joseph Smith. In imp:Aching the character of Joseph Smith he had no desire to cast any reflections on tie honesty and integrity of those who followed him. He Submitted that Joseph Smith was an imposter in that he made claims for which there was no justification and pretended to be one thing while in reality he *as another. Mr. Ward then traced the career of Smith, contending that there was no evidence to corroborate his assertion that heavenly visitants told him that he was to become the instrument in the bands of God for the salvation of humanityand directed him where to find the plates of the Book of Mormon I which was to be the new Bible. Commenting ion the book itself Mr. Ward said that so far as his knowledge of literature was concerned, human dulness never produced a greater mass of sleep-producing nonsense. There was not a particle of corroborative historical evidence for a single one of its absurd statements, and Mr. Ward held that with a large amount of natural cunning Smith made up the Book of Mormon by a very liberal use of his own vivid imagination and a large number of , passages from the Old and New Testaments. Smith Iconstantly obtained money by false pretences, alleging that God commanded him to ask the members of his I Church to put into the church treasury all the money J they had beyond that which was absolutely necessary t for their existence. Smith's love for idleness was • actually gratified by a revelation Sanctioning it. Mr. Ward summed up Smith's career by stating that he was an imposter trading upon the credulity and superstition 1 of mankind.

Mr. ftt-srtwortrti said Mr. Ward was labouring under a mistake in regard to his (Mr. Rushworth's) identity. He claimed that Joseph Smith was the founder of that sect of Mormons who were located in Salt Lake City. He (the speaker) disclaimed all association with those people, for that was not the Mormonism.. founded by Joseph Smith. He quoted legal decisions substantiating this statement and also read extracts from the book of Mormon forbidding polygamy. Mr. Ward, he said, had quoted from the books of the Utah Mormon church which he (Mr. Rushworth) repudiated. Joseph Smith should be measured by the books of the church for which he was :espousible and not by a spurious misrepre- sentation of his teaching and practices. The Book of Mormon did not claim to be a new Bible but an inde- pendent and supplementary record—a confirmatory witness to the Bible itself—and he contended that the church of Latter Day Saints was the nearest approach to the Christianity of Jesus Christ. As to the state- ment that Smith obtained money by false pretences, Smith lived and died a poor man, and the money given to the Church was not used for his aggrandise- ment but to provide for the widows and orphans and the poor. As to the revelations of God to Joseph Smith, the only evidence they had was their belief in the integrity and honesty of the man together with the , cumulative evidence of their own personal intelligence as they had put into practice the teaching given through his instrumentality. Joseph Smith did not preach or practice polygamy and the documentary and traditional evidence bore out that statement. The question as to ,whether Joseph Smith was 9. I polygamist was stronglz debated. Another subject round which the discussion waged was as to whether independent witnesses had seen the plates of the Book of Mormon, Mr. Rushworth producing authorities which Mr. Ward disputed. . (A report of Tuesday's debate will be found on page 2.)

JACK TAR'S HARDSHIPS.

Twenty Years' Suffering from Piles

Ended by Zam-Buk.

Arduous duti.s and exposnre on one of H.n. battle- ships caused qr. Alfred Thompson. of GO, Chetwode- street, Crewe. to suffer many years with piles. lie has made public the story of his curs by Zam-link through a " Crewe Chronicle" reporter. Mr. Thompson said:— "I was often on night duty, and many a time had to sit in the lifeboat for hours at a stretch, wet or fine, foul or fair. Sometimes we went to aid a vessel in distress, and sometimes searched for a man overboard, -and were frequently out in the wet boat all night, unable to regain the ship. As a consequence I began to suffer agonising pains, heat, and irritation from piles. I could hardly walk, let alon e work, and was accused by my shipmates of laziness, and shirking my duty ! "As time went on the torture got so unendurable that I applied for sick leave. Despite all the ship's doctor's medicines and ointments, the relief I got VMS only temporary, and I soon got as incl as ever. I suffered from the piles for twenty years, long after I settled down on shore, and then one day my attention was drawn to Zam-Buk. The first application cooled the inflammation and alleviated the pain. After per- severing with the dressings for a short time I was able to walk the length of the street without the aid of my stick, a feat I had been unable to y rfcrm before. The pains, swellings and itching gradually vanished under the soothing influence of Zam-Buk, and after using a second box all traces of the piles had disappeared. I have never &rice been troubled with them." Zam-Buk is the standard soothing and curative halm. Every home reeds a box.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

YOUTHFUL THIEVES.

Walter Harris (9), 15, Pierpoint-street • David Algie (9), 96, Pierpoint-street, and William Griffiths (11), 102, Pierpoint-street, were charged with stealing from the shop doorway of Mr. Bowdon, Buttermarket-street, on Friday, a pair of boots, of the value of 2s. 61c1. All pleaded guilty.—The Chief Constable stated that Mr. Bowdon saw the boys loitering about his shop window, and subsequently missed the boots. The boys took the boots to Mr. Tyrer's pawnshop, and Mr. Bracegirdle,his assistant, who had several times been complimented by the Bench, would not accept them and gave information to Detective Metcalfe, who arrested the boys.—Harris (who had been birched previously for theft). was ordered to receive six strokes of the birch rod, and Algie and Griffiths were dealt with under the First Offenders Act, the Bench expressing the hope that the boys' parents would inflict suitable punishment.

WHEN TRAVELLING.

An appetite in the train. Everyone knows it. If is not appeased at the right time with something light and satisfying, you grow faint and irritable. You take no interest in either your book or the scenery. The conversation annoys you and you arrive at your journey's end with no relish for food. Some thin slices of White or Hovis currant bread would have kept things all right, being light, delicious and nearly all nutriment. That famous physician, the late Sir William Gull, thought so highly of currants that he always advised his patients when on a long journey to carry plum pudding. Currant bread contains about 30 per cent, of currants—the same proportions as in plum pudding.

RE'. HERBERT DUNNICO.

Important Charge Offered.

The following announcement appeared in the "Liver- pool Daily Post" :—The members of Kehsington Baptist Church, Jubilee-drive, Liverpool, at a special church meeting held a few days ago, considered the re- commendations of the deacons as to the new minister to this church. There was a large attendance of members , and Mr. Lancaster, one of the deacons, presided. The, ' church secretary (Mr. John Bennett) stated that be had convened that meeting according to the church con- stitution as to election of a pastor, and he would make known the decision of the Board, who, after prayerful consideration, asked the church members to give the Rev. Herbert Donnie°, of I Warrington, a call to the pastorate, and that unanimously. There had been every opportunity taken to hear good men, and, apart from the personal knowledge of Mr. Donnie°, and his power as a preacher and lecturer, he was highly recommended by the Rev. John Thomas, of Myrtle-street. Dr. kked had also stated in a communication :—"Mr. Dunnico's coming to this city would be a great service to the Free Church movement, and to the cause of righteousness generally."—The members freely spoke in favour of the nomination, and each deacon added a word of testimony, and the call was given unanimously. The Church at Kensington is one of the leading churches in the denomination. The building has 1,200 sittings, the church roll contains between 500 and 600 names and there are nearly 900 scholars in the Sunday school. The pastorate has been vacant since December of last year when the Rev. E. E. Walter ex-moderator of the Union retired after a pastorate of 34 years. The" call was given unanimously at one of the largest church meetings ever held. A large number of congratulatory letters have been sent urging Mr. Dunnico to accept. Dr. Aked says " I am very happy in the prospect of having you for a neighbour, a colleague and friend near aChand. Your coining to Liverpool will bring me a personal pleasure as well as worlds of help to good causes. It is a great thing for you to come to Liverpool so early, it is a One opening, the prospects are exceedingly bright, founda- tions good, and you will have a good career. I rejoice in your prospects, for your own sake and that of all the movements with which my life is hound." Prior to the sermon on Sunday evening Mr. Dunnico intimated to the crowded congregation that he had received a unanimous call to the pastorate at Liverpool. They would recognize at once that such an important offer demanded the most careful and prayerfnl consideration but he hoped to be able to announce bis decision on Sunday next. . . . .

Mr. bunnico who is 29 years of age, is an alumni, of Nottingham University and the Midland Baptist College, the alma mater of Drs. Clifford, Aked, Dawson, Burns, etc. Duribg his course at Nottingham he was awarded the Hewby Scholarship which is worth £5O a year. Ill- health, however, prevented him from taking full advan- tage of this. The rev. gentleman settled at Golborne- street in July 1902 and was ordained in October of the same year and amongst those who took part were— Professors Bumby, 8.A., Bowser, 8.D., Revs. Dr. Aked, T. E. Roberts, M.A., H. V.Thoruas and others. Letters were also read from Drs. McLaren and Clifford. Since then Mr. Dunnico has won for himself a position of influence, and has gathered around him hosts of friends. His decision will be awaited with keen interest.

CHESHIRE EDUCATION ESTIMATES. The Rates That Will Be Required.

The annual meeting of the Cheshire Education Com- mittee was held at Crewe on Monday. Mr. C. E. Thornycroft was re-appointed chairman and Dr. Hodgson deputy chairman. Some discussion took place on a proposal 'that the committee should discuss the Education Bill.—Colonel Dixon, the chairman of the County Council, appealed to the committee not to discuss the hill as discussion might result in harm, seeing that the committee had settled the religious question in Cheshire..

Colonel Thorpe expressed a similar opinion. Judge Bradbury said that, while be agreed with what Colonel Dixon had said,there were questions of adminis- tration which the Committee ought to dismiss A vital and pernicious change with regard to securing uni- formity and co-ordination was contained in the pro- posal for the transference of powers in clause 26 of the Act.

Dr. Hodgson hoped that Colonel Dixon would not move a resolution. It was of vital importance that they should maintain the organisation which they had set up and which had been copied by a number of other connties,and which was not affected by the hill. Eventually it was decided that the Committee should have a special meeting to discuss the bill. The scheme for providing a secondary school for Macclesfield was brought up. It was pointed out that the original estimate of the cost worked out at ED per head whereas the cost of the scheme as now presented was £6O. Judge Bradbury said he thought the Com- mittee ought to pause before they laid down this scale for the whole county.—Dr. Hodgson defended the estimste, but the plans were referred back to the architect with an instruction to try and reduce the cost.

The annual estimates for, the county were submitted. They showed an expenditure for elementary educat,on of £212,282 and for higher education of £69,507. The Finance Committee recommended rates of Bd. in the pound for elementary education purposes, and 2d. in the pound for higher education purposes.

WARRINGTON.

The New Post Office. The site of the post office to be erected on the east side of Springfield-street is being boarded in, and the work of preparing the land for the erection of the building has already commenced. Death of Mr. Robert Cook. Another respected Warringtonian passed away on Thursday last in the person of Mr. Robert Cook, Wilderspool Causeway. The deceased gentleman, wha was 83 years of age, had been in failing health for some time. He was an ardent supporter of the Co-operative movement and was long connected with the Warrington Co-operative Society, having acted as a member of the committee. The funeral took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Sunday afternoon when there acre many evidences of the respect and I esteem in which the deceased was held. A large Inumber of floral tributes were placed over the grave. Warrington Widow's Suicide. An inquest was conducted by Ur. S. Brighouse at the Police station on Monday, tonchinsr the death of Ann Mallon, (63), widow, 61, Foundry-street. —The evidence showed that early on Monday morning deceased's son, who is an engine driver, went to work, leaving her asleep on the sofa. When at 7.45 a daughter of deceased got up, Mrs. Mallon had die- appearea from the house. A search was made and deceased was found banging from a beam in an out- building. When cut down by a man named Michael Kelly she was quite dead. Of late she had been depressed and strange in her manner.—A verdict of "Suicide whilst of unsound mind" was returned. UNDER THR CLOCK for Good floors.—Advt. Fatal " Jacksharping." _ . . .

. _ A sad drowning fatality was inquired into by Mr. S. Brighouse, c runty coroner, and a jury at Warrington on Monday.—The evidence showed that on Friday afternoon Sananel Griffiths, the nine year old son of an ironworker living at 76, Edwin-street, was catching jacksharpa from the St. Helens and Sankey Canal along with three companions when he overbalanced and fell into the water. The other boys could not reach him and be sank before assistance was forth- coming. The body was recovered by Constable Woods.—The cirumstances of the fatality were related to the jury by George Henry Oilier (11), 43, Plumpton-street, and a verdict of " Accidently, drowned " was returned. Death of Mr. Alfred Lamplugh. The death is announced of Mr. Alfred Lamplugh, which took place at the residence of his son, Rokeby Rectory, Yorkshire, on Saturday. Up to a short time ago the deceased gentleman, who was 82 years of age, lived at Stockton Heath, when he appeared to be enjoying; remarkably glad health for one who had reached such an advanced age. Many years ago he carried on an extensive business in Horaelnarket- street as a draper, rae. He was prominently identified with Wesleyaa Methodism, and had held most of the offices it is lathe power of the denomination to confer on a layman: The funeral took place at Thelwall churchyard on Tuesday afternoon in the presence of a large gathering of relatives and friends. ST. PAUL'S CHURCH.

Successful Sale of Work.

Gratifying success attended the annual sale of work in connection with St. Paul's Church which was held in the Assembly Room at the Conservative Club on Mon- day. The ladies of the parish had entered into the arrangements with the greatest enthusiasm and the result was that the stalls were very heavily laden with useful and fancy wares. Throughout the afternoon and evening there was a contineus stream of visitors,and the proceeds amounted to the handsome sum of .£410.95. 3d. which will be devoted to various parochial objects. The sale was opened by Lady Grrsnall in the presence of a large gathering. The Rev. W. Bracecamp (Vicar) presided and was supported by the Rev. R. FL Bowen (curate), Messrs. J. H. Smethurst and J. R+gby (wardens). _ _

The CHAIRMAN said many of them would no doubt be surprised to find themselves in the Conservative I Club on that occasion. That was the first time they had had to leave their own schools for twenty-four years. They were an old established firm—(laughter) —and they could not say they had left because of strc A widening or decline of business, but they had left simply because they could not help themselves. They were pleased to see such a large gathering and hoped all those present would give tbem generous support. They knew the position at St. Paul's perfectly well. They raised a great deal of money but they needed it all. They were only sure of £l2B to start the year with and the remainder of the funds had to be raised in some way, ono of the methods being that sale of work. That annual event bed grown ycar by year until lest year they realized £315. They wanted to increase that surer on the present occasion because they had the church to clean and that would mean an ex- penditure of at least £SC3. They bad also to improve the lighting and hea,ing apparatus, so that their requirements were considerable. In introducing Lady Greenall the Vicar paid a tribute to her generosity to every parish in VVarrington. pointing out that probably very few realized the good work she did. (Applause.) Lady GREENALL said that though the fact that the schools were not at their disposal for the sale was regrettable, she hoped it would not have any adverse influence but would act as a Lpur to a willing home, and that when the proceeds were countc I they would prove to be far in advance of previous years.(Applause). On the motion of Mr. J. H. SMETHURST seconded by Mr. J. RIGBY and supported by Mr. R. H. BOWEN, a very cordial vote of thanks WPS passed to Lady Greenall and the business of the sale commenced.

--, —.- The stalls were most attractively arranged, the following ladies acting as stall holders : No. I.—Mrs. Gibson Smith, Mrs. Lord and Miss Dodie Reid.

No. 2.—The Misses Geddes, Mrs. Hepherd, and Mira' Helen Burgesl. No. 3.—Mesdames Bracecamp, Bowen, Lindley, T. Locker, Cox, H. Jolley, and W. Smith. No. 4 (Sweet Stall).—Miss Dorothea Bracecamp and Miss Winifred Davenport„(Shelfield)..

Mr DEAR CHILDREN, 'tt) At last the spring has arrived and the cold winds are going. How pleasant it is to see the country turning green again, after the long months of winter. It makes one quite eager to be in the open air, enjoying the sun- shine and warmth. Well, we shall really begin to think of the summer holidays at the seaside or on the river. Everywhere the spring flowers are coming into bloom, wallflowers and hyacinths and a host of other beautiful flowers. I shall be pleased to award a prize to the boy and girl who send me the best essay on "Spring." These must be sent to me at the office of this paper by May 19th. You will find it quite an easy matter to write a short article on "Spring." I hope all Who compete will remember the rules, namely, to write neatly, on one side of the paper only, and in INE. Any one not following out these regulations will be disqualified. Please put your age ou each competition,. as I cannot expect the younger members to write as well as the older ones.

I have a prize waiting for Annie Rowley, for her contribution,-but she sent me no address. As soon as I receive this the prize shall be sent off. With all good wishes from

UNCLE NED.

DEAR UNCLE NED,—Thank you very much for the box of *chocolates, which I received this dinner-time. Imagine my glad surprise when I came home frot.o school and found a parcel waiting for me. I am very glad you have accepted my contribution, Els I want very much to see it in print, and I hope later on to be able to send you something better still. I am sure the columns of "The Children's Corner" are very interesting, and as I devote most of my spare time to reading, it always affords me great pleasure every Friday in reading them. 1 always rush for the "Children's Corner" directly I see the paper; indeed, I am generally so much interested in the former that I have no time for the other. pages. Now I must close, hoping you will--Spend a happy Easter. I remain, your loving niece, 0 ,FAITH MEWETT.

DEAR UNCLE NED,—I think that you will think one of your nieces has left your League altogether; but I have not. I am sending you a short story. I hope/you will like it. It is called "The Name Pussy." With much love, from your loving niece, ROSE ENGLISH.

Did you ever think why we call the ,cat "Puss'"? Long ago the people of Egypt, *ho had many idol-goda, worshipped the cat. They, thooght she was like the moon, because she was more bright at night, and because her eyes change juat as the moon changes, which is 'sometimes full and sometimes only the bright crescent, or the half moon. So these beople made an. idol with the c'at's head, and named it Pasht,, the came name they gave to the moon, for the word means the "lace of• the moon." That word has been changed to "pas" or "pus," and has come at last to be "puss," the name which almost every one gives to the cat. Puss and pussy-cat are pet names for Kitty eferywhere. Who ever thought of it as given to her thousands of yeais ago, and that then the people bowed down and prayed to her?

IF MY SPADE STICKS IN THE TREE, I WILL FOLLOW YOU.

More than four hundred years ago there was a labourer in Italy named S'..forza Attendolo. One day he was digging in his garden, when some soldiers came by, on their way to the wars, and tried to persuade him to join them, promising him all sorts of spoil and plunder. Sforza could not make up his mind; the promises of the soldiers we tempting, and yet he did not like leaving his tiny cottage. At last he said, "My spade shall decide for me. I will throw it up into yonder tree; if it sticks in the tree I will follow you, but if it falls I will remain here."

-le threw the spade, and it stuck fast in the Jranches, and so Sforza marched off with the soldiers. • A terrible war was then raging in Italy, and Sforza fought valiantly. He was such a brave and honourable man that he soon rose to great power, and became a noted general, honoured by all for his bravery, uprightness, and mercy. He had great possessions given him as a reward for his valour. The fate of old Sforza was a sad one, though in all ways worthy of his noble life. One day a favourite page fell into the river, whereupon Sforza sprang in to save him, but the current was too strong for him, for he was by this time an old man, and he was swept away by, the stream and drowned.

DEAR UNCLE NED,—I hope yOD will be pleased with my story I am sending for com- petition:—

PAYING A VISIT. A country boy once visited his uncle in a large town. As he was walking down a street, , a savage dog cams out of a pa.ssage and tried to bite him. The box turned round to find a stone to throw at the brute, but he could see nothing but pebbles tightly rammed in the ground. At last he said, "This is an odd place. All the stones are fastened'up, and all the dogs let .loose."—I am, your faithful nephew, GORDON PENN.

DEAR UNCLE UNCLE NED,—I Wiell to become a mem- ber of The Children's Corner League. lam eleven years of age, and I am in standard six at school. Never in all my life did I see an uncle with so many nephews and nieoes as you have. We receive the paper on Saturday, and I have a look at the part which has the Children's Corner in, before we have dinner. For many weeks I have thought of becoming a member, but I have not had time. Next week I may send you a story which you will like I think. I am in perfect health at present and I hope you are the same. Yours truly, H. DUEBIIII.Y.

THE SPARROW'S NEST. Nay, only look what I lave found— A sparrow's nest upon the ground; A sparrow's nest, as you may see, Blown out of yonder old elm tree. • And what a medley thing it is; I never saw a nest like this. How neatly woven with tender care, Of silvery moss and shining hair. Here is a scrap of red and brown, Like an old washerwoman's gown; And here is muslin' pink and green, And bits 'of calico between. See, hair of dog and fur of cat, And fragments of a worsted mat, . And shreds of Silk and many a feather, All knit so cunningly together.

—From MILLIE SMITIL

ALIGHTING FROM THE TRAIN

In London there are trains running under- ground, and some of these make a tour of the City, stopping at frequent intervals at the different stations. The traveller cannot fail to notice one thing in connection with these trains: they stop but a very short time, and passengers wishing to get out at a station must lose no time, or they will find their train off again and their chance of Alighting is lost. A. very stout lady was once travelling in one of the underground trains. Presently a gentle- man entered the carriage, and noticed that the lady seemed, somewhat troubled. He. asked what was the matter, when she replied: "The next station at which we arrive will be Sloane Square, and I shall be so glad if you will be good enough to assist me to leave the carriage on our arrival. I have already been twice round London, having unsuccessfully made attempts to leave the train. Being heavy and clumsy in my movements, I find it easier to descend from the doorway backwards, and I have twice been occupied in this endeavour when a porter, thinking that I was entering the carriage, addressing me, said, Now, Missf, be quick; the train is off," and then gave me a push and forced me Into the carriage again." —From OLIVE M. KLNGDON.

A few yeara ago the idea or anyone cycling in the ordinary' garments of everyday business life would have seemed so incongruous as to be almost laughable to cyclists of those days. Now the utility of the bicycle is so widely recognized that it is quite a common sight to meet top.- hatted business men in the ;morning riding from their semi-rural suburban villas to the railway station, -Id at night using the same ready means of getting the railway home again.

A Cycle Campers' Demonstration.

The devotees of cycle camping make no secret of their methods; and seem most kuixious to spread a knowledge of "how it's done." The recent demonstration given at a lecture hall in West Kensington proved both entertaining and instruc- tive. About a dozen frail-looking tents were pitched, illustrating two or three different patterns. The A tent is very light and suitable for one or two men, while other patterns are Nknown as the Gipsy and the Cottage tents, respectively. The latter is described as suitable for a married couple, or several men. During the evening a quartet of campers gave a demonstration of pitching a camp, preparing a meal, retiring to sleep, and strikingcamp. Including unpacking., packitg up the numerous small items of equipment, and re-strap- ping the kit on to bicycles, the entire operation was performed in 34 minutes.- Such dexterity and division of labour must have been the result of diligent rehearsal.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
The New Road Club.

It is stated that the number of members already enrolled by the Road Club of the British Isles exceeds all anticipations. The club has been formed •to meet the requirements of motorists, driving tourists, and hunting and coaching people. Its objects are to provide comfortable country quarters at convenient points on the great main roads and elsewhere, with first-class stabling and properly equipped motor garages, replete with stocks of petrol, tyres, and other requisites. Lord Montague of Beaulieu is the president, and particulars can be obtained of Mr. Eden Paget, 8, Clarges Street, Piccadilly, W. Although the new club cannot expect support from the democratic ranks of cycling, wheelmen will watch its operations with sympathetic interest.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
The Spread of Variable Gears.

There is no longer any doubt that the variable gear has come to stay. The advance this year in the popularity of two-speed gears is very marked. Whereas last season the New Hudson Company was the only important firm to offer a double- speeded bicycle at the popular price of ten guineas, this season has brought into line no fewer than seven other manufacturers of the first rank following more or less heartily the New Hudson lead. Two- speed gears, like the Hub, which has endured many seasons of ,severe testing, may be regarded as absolutely reliable. Their efficiency and dura- bility are removed from the region ,of speculation. A rider with a two-speed gear may face any journey with the confidence that neither hills nor head winds will make him repine. With his low speed to fall back upon in times of need, his average pace will afford him satisfaction at his ,journey's end.

HULL v. ST. HELENS.

The season closed at Hull with the meeting of Hull and St. Helens on the Boulevard Ground. The match had u lively opening, for within ten minutes the home team profited by a free kick, Goddard putting them two points ahead. This was followed by a further score almost inimrdiately, for Parry, taking a pass from Cook, crossed in the coiner. Goddard only just failed fit goal. Before half time Pullen took the ball from Goddard and got over under the Posts, for the same player to add the goal points,bringing the half time score to ten points to nil,. On resuming the home team got straight down from' the kick-off, and Carroll crossed over in the corner. Harrison tried the goal kick but failed. It was a runaway victory all through for hull. ,Carvill roused enthusiasm by the way he dodged opponent after opponent, and finally fought his way over the line ; and another unconverted try was scored by Jenkins, Goddard failing to improve either. Result : —Hull, 2 goals 5 tries (19 points), St. Helens, nil.

Perhaps it was hardly to be expected that the clericals would welcome the Bill, though it: is gratifying to see that some eminent members of the Church of England have shown a desire to accept the Compromise. A good deal Of wild talk is being indulged in to the effect that the teaching in the schools will be "Noncon- formist." Of course, the fact is that the Cowper-Temple Teaching is now being given in every Council School, and can only be called Nonconformist if we are to believe that the Bible is a Nonconformist book. The very name indicates the origin of the -compromise, for Mr. Cowper-Temple was a Churchman, and the clause in the Act of 1870 which bears his name was strongly resisted by the Nonconformists at that time.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
TUB NEW EDUCATION BILL.

The subject uppermost in the minds of most Free Churchmen is, of course, the new Educa- tion Bill introduced by Mr. Birrell. The for- mation stage of the new measure was watched with some anxiety in Nonconformist circles, and there was considerable relief when the pro- visions of the Bill were made known. The Bill carries out in a just and equitable way the prin- ciples to which the Government and the majority in the House of Commons were pledged. It is absurd, for the measure to be called a Nonconformist Bill, for not a single line favours Free Churchmen more than any other class of citizens. All are treated on the same basis, with the exception, perhaps, of the clauses which provide denominational teaching, and these are concessions, not to the Free Churches, but to Anglicans ,and Roman Catholics.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
NATIONAL COUNCIL OPINION.

The opinion of the Free Churches is focussed in the resolution passed by the General Com- mittee of the National Free Church Council, which met after Mr. Birrell had introduced his Bill. The meeting had an exceptionally lai.ge attendance, and Mr. John Massie, M.P., moved, and Dr. John Clifford seconded, a reselution heartily welcoming the new Bill " as a sincere attempt to establish a national and just-system of State education, and as going far towards carrying out the will of the people as expressed at the General Election." " The Committee," says the resolution "pledges its loyal and strenuous support to the Government in carry- ing the main principles of the Measure into law. It reserves its right, however, to criticise the de- tailed provisions of the Bill when they bre fully known. In its judgment some of the proposals are open to serious objection, such, for instance, as the implication of the teacher in the facilities for sectarian instruction in the four-fifths schools; while certain matters of great impor-. tame and urgency—for •example, the securing of a national system of training colleges—are in- sufficiently dealt with. On these and other matters the Committee will at a later stage seek an opportunity of. pressing its views upon His Majesty's Government."

===== 0002601_19060428_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
"NONCONFORMIST trACHING."

OPEN-AIR WORK.

In Free Church Council circles the prospects of Open-Air Work are being discussed, and it is felt that this year great efforts should be made in the way of organised services in the open. An interesting development is promised with the Metropolitan Federation. For some time past experiments have been made to find the Most suitable form of portable pulpit for Open-Air Work. 1 Mr. Samuel Cooke has generously provided the means for these experiments, and no has Presented to the Metrouolitan Federation six Open-air pulpits: These pulpits will be for use in the parks in London. It is proposed to arrange for the co-operation of the various Councils adjacent to the parks so that united work may lie undertaken; the pulpits will be put at the disposal of these Councils.

MISSION TAN.

Plans for Mission work are well in hand. The Mission ,Van of the National Free Church Council will again be in evidence, under the charge of the Bev. Jesse Mayo. The work will commence at , the beginning- of May with the Dorset and South Somerset Federation. A start will be made at Chard, and the van will work up through Yeovil and Gillingham into the district of the Wilts and East Somerset Federation. The villages touched by the Councils of Salisbury, Warminster, and Malmesbury will be visited. It is expected that this work will last about three months; after this it is probable that the van will go to the Bristol district.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
SUNDAY OBSERVANCE. •

The Sunday Observance question, which was prominent at the annual meetings of the National Free Church Council, is to be the subject of a special Conference, at the Caxton Hall, on Wednesday, May 9.. This National Conference is being called by a joint committee composed of leading Anglicans, Free Church- men, and labour men. A letter has just been issued by Rev. J. Scott Lidgett, M.A.,. Presi- dent, and Rev. Thomas Law, General Sec'reta.ry of the National Free Church Council, to the General Committee of the National Council, in- viting them ,to co-operate at the Conference, which will be presided over by the Archbishop of Canterbury. This Conference is to be the inauguration of a groat National Movement to safeguard the Day of Rest.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
RATING OF CHURCHES.

The dismissal of the appeal against the rating of Dr. Clifford's church creates a situation which can only be remedied by fresh legislation. The Organising and Legal Committees of the National Free Church Council are to meet on an early/ date in order to frame a Bill for the alteration of the law of rating as far as it applies to churches. It is expected that such a 'Bill will be introduced into the House of Commons by a Free Church M.P. • •

VEMCE TOOK Volt FREE CHURCHMEN.

An invaluable referace book for Free Church- men has just been issued in the 1906 volume of the Free Church Year Book. This contains, the full official account of the Birmingham Meet- ings, a review of the year's work, and valuable information for Free Churchmen. In the statistical field we notice the striking inter- national figures for the last few years. 1111896, for inatance, the number of communicants in the Free Churches was 16,625,-152. This has now grown to 21,37.1,054, whilst in the Anglican Church the figures have been 3,122,526 for 1896, aid 8,742,019 for last year.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. A. J. Jolley and the Education Committee.

Sir,—ls it not an insult to the ratepayers for the co-opted members of the Education Committee to persist in forcing Mr. A. J. Jolley upon them as a representative? Mr. Jolley's record on the Committee and on the Board of Guardians is known to the whole town, and there can be no question as to the opinion of the property-owners and ratepayers generally on the subject. It is to be hoped the Council will put the matter right when it comes before them on Tuesday next, as -even the Education Committee should have some regard for the people who pay the rates.—Yours MARK TAPLEY. April 27th 190 G.

" And It Came to Pass."

Sir,—l was much interested in reading through Mr E. Osborne's article that appeared in your last week's issue under the above heading. , say I was interested, but I cannot say that I was altogether surprised, as anyone who has followed Mr. Osborne's political career of recent times must have noticed his Socialistic leanings. Now what I should like to know is, are we to take Mr. Osborne's article " And it Came to Pass," as a dis- avowal of Liberalism and his future adherence entirely to Socialism and to the Socialist Party ? This, per- haps, is a rather blunt way of putting the question. Still, I think it much better to be straightforward in these matters, as then we are not so apt to be misunder- stood, or our intentions misconstrued. In writing this letter it is not my intention to be critical towards Mr. Osborne or his writings, but it would certainly "clear the air " for many of your readers if Mr. Osborne would kindly answer the above question.—Yours

L. WELLINGS.

Southern-street, Stockton Heath, April 24th, 1906. - _ San Francisco Disaster.—The Whitham Family. Sir,—Tn answer to the many inquiries that have been made relative to the safety of the Henry Whin= family during the desolations made last week in the city of San Francisco both by earthquake and fire, allow me to say that we have many good reasons for believing them to be safe and sound. We have followed the reports of the shock and flame, and have felt in a measure consoled for them. The flames, surely, have not reached their home within half- a-mile. They approached it from two quarters at one time, hut on the Mission-street side they failed to come within three-quarters of a mile, and on the Vann Ness- avenue side with half-a-mile of the Whitham residence. Living, as our friends did, in a house built of wood, the earthquake could not destroy it; nor could there be any serious danger of heavy materials falling upon the inmates of the house. The plaster on the wals mightfall; also the' pictures and mnrial ornaments; but the walls would remain intact,even though the brick foundations -on which they rested' were broken by the earthquake. Had the earthquake occurred during business hours, instead of fire o'clock a.m., Miss Ruby Whitham and Miss Dora Whitham might have been hurt by the ruin of the beautiful new office building of the Southern Pacific Railroad Company. Mr. Whitham would be at work during the night of the seismic tremor : but the wood and iron structure in which be worked justifies out presqmption that he is safe from serious harm. If the building took fire later, doubtless be bud time to make good his escape. We have received letters this week from California, describing events eight and ten days before the desolations. Although we have not received any information from Mr. Whitham since the earthquake, our hone is good. I am in correspondence with the United States Consul of Cardiff, relative to his security ; and shall be pleased to forward to your readers any information that may come to me next week.—Yours,

'JOSEPH ELLISON. Ynmhwl 13aptist Church, Pontypridd; Glam. April 260, 1906.

Association Football. Sir,--in your last issue a further letter appears from "Full Back "—in answer to mine of the previous week- in which I am now charged with twisting his first letter with its simple question into a denunciation of the District Association but I will leave it to the intelligence of your render § to judge as to who is doing the twisting and juggling. If it is of any satisfaction to " Full Back " I admit in the first instance that not being sufficiently known to the football community in the Warrington District I am replying to his correspondence for the sole purpose of bringing my name before the public, for self adver- tisement. Modesty with me is a thing of the past. In fact, modesty, in football management is an unknown quantity. " Full Back " objects to the question being dealt with by paid officials, yet in the latter part of his letter he suggests that I might be of some assistance, but if it is unfair for paid officials to reply to his correspon- dence, it is equally unfair for paid officials to render assistance. Full Back "may be modest, but is hardly consistent. In " Full Back's " first letter be asks for "an expression of opinion from those who take an interest in " Association Football and its charms," but think that he will agree that the expressions of , opinion are a lone time coming, as save for a reply from a paid official his request has received little con- sideration from your readers. Having explained why a club cannot be formed under the name of Warrington A.F.C." " Full Back " now states " that a suitable name does not matter much as 1 long as the players are all right." Even "the colours and field and things can be arranged afterwards," but "Full Back "will find that when a Club is registedeven the field and colours are of equal importance to the players. 1 If the formation of a new club is to he an accom- plished fact " Full Back " may rest assured that the matter will have to be taken up by outsiders and as the , constitution of Leagues and Combinations for next season will be decided within the next three weeks he had better forget his modesty and proceed to business. I have already explained the mode of procedure for the formation of a new Club and "if a representative com- mittee void of cliques will assure a successful club being formed," I see no reason for further delay. If the players are able and willing to pay their fair share of the expenses of a dinner, why not proceed with the dinner ? As to his pertinent questions : Let me in the first place inform " Full Back " that we are in no way responsible to individuals for our actions, though I will give your readers the informa- tion he desires. The reply to the first question is "six with representatives of the Press," and to the second "Yes."—Yours truly, T. P. BRADSHAW, Secretary, Warrington and District F. A.

MR. A. H. CROSFIELD M.P., AND THE TRADES COUNCIL.

A REPLY FROM GREECE.

The Trades Disputes Bill and Chinese

Labour

Mr. A. H. Crosfield 11. P., has favoured us with the following copy of a letter he has written to Councillor Stott (secretary of the Warrington Trades Council) in reply to a resolution paAsecl by that body. Hotel de la Grande Bretagne, Athens. April 20th, 1906.

Councillor F. Stott, Secretary, Trades and Labour Council. 160, Battersby-lane, Warrington. Dear Sir, I am duly in receipt of your courteous letter of the 11th, informing me of the Resolution, adopted unanimously at a meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council on the 10th, namely:— "That I be instructed to write asking if you will kindly inform me why you did not vote for the "Trades Disputes Bill" which was introduced by Mr. Hudson, M.P., on Friday March 30th." I thought you and your friends would perhaps have been aware of the fact that I " Paircd "in support of the Government a few weeks since. At a representa- tive meeting of the Progressive Labour League held on March 19th, I told my friends there of my intended absence for a short time during the Session, and they were kind enough to raise no objection. Perhaps you missed the reports of my remarks on that occasion and have been misled by some of the Tory scribes who, finding: Tory measures and policy at a very heavy dis- count just now, think their best plan from a Party point of view is to shun measures and go for men, more particularly myself ! If it amuses them it does not hurt me, but still I must say it would be better if they had the pluck to sign their names to their letters, and the candour to stick to facts. As they do neither the ' one nor the other I have thought it really was not worth while answering letters of this sort, and Press paragraphs of a similar description. But at the meeting of the Progressive Labour League, to which I have referred, I ventured to ask the members of that League- to whom I owe so great a debt to take cum granu salis the distinctly disingenuous attempts that have been made of late to distort one's acts and motives,and therefore to thoroughly mislead the constituency. May I frankly ask the same favour from your friends and yourself? Front the inter- course we have had together lam sure you will not refuse it. _ _

For instance it has been insinuated that owing to my alleged interests as an employer I have sought to shirk the question of "Trades Disputes —a grotesque misrepresentation and no mistake, as the facts prove, and after all an ounce of fact is worth a ton of insinua- tion. The very first thing I did at Westminster was to ballot for the Trades Disputes Bill. Had I been lucky in the draw I bad promised to give my day to certain able representatives of Labour. I hope and think the members of the Trades and Labour council will thoroughly approve of my action iu the matter. A word of warning about another question on which your members, like &her operatives of Warrington in general feel stronger --Chinese Servile Labour. The most persistent attempts have been made, and still are made to convey the impression that because in order to address the Junior Liberals on a Friday some time since,' had paired in advance for that date ; and because when the tie came I was confined by illness to my hotel, and unable to attend either the meeting in Warrington, or the secind day of debate on this question, therefore I had failed to support the ' Government on a question which materially affected the result of the election. Now without wishing in the least to escape the , responsibility of voting myself on the complex ques- tions ahead of us in these days of constructive legislation, I did point out at the time in Warrington that as things stand, and with our enormous majority in the House, a " Pair " on a Division was in fact tantamount to a double measure of support to the Government, and that the charge made by the " Observer " newspaper was therefore contrary to fact. The Editor of the "Examiner" a little later suggested that under the circumstances a polite withdrawal and apology would be the natural sequel. Apparently the responsible people on the Tory side take a different view of what is fitting in political matters of this kind. As I see the proceedings of the Trades and Labour Council are published, and to avoid any further mis- apprehension,l am sure,that as at this distance I have not had an opportunity of consulting you on the point, you will be quite willing for me to send copies of this letter to the Press. I need not prolong it by dwelling , an the attitude and sympathies of the Tory party on both the big questions about which I have written you : they are thoioughly anti-democratic, and there is the strongest feeling on the Tory side in Parliament. and among our Conservative friends out of it, because the Liberal Government will not assent to any species of slavery in the conditions of labour under the British flag. I feel as strongly now as I did during the campaign that the introduction of this Chinese labour under the conditions that have obtained in South Africa has been nothing less than an abomina- tion. As in other cases, so in this, the present Government has had a difficult legacy to deal with and I think in every particular they have handled it with a wisdom and discretion that must command the general approval of the electorate. If I may take this opportunity in writing you and your friends to allude in one word to two other ques- tions of the first imp .rtance which the Government' have to deal with, I hope the membersof your Council and yourself thoroughly approve in principle of the Government's action in regard to Natal, and on the complicated yet infinitely importi,nt question of educa- tion that confronts them. I am here to see the first few opening days of these , ancient international games that have now been rev i and of which I hope to send some little account to the Warrington press on my return North. Pray express my acknowledgements to the members of your Council for the courtesy of their communica- tion and believe me.—Very faithfully yours, A. IL CUOS

COSTUME AND COON CONCERT.

A highly successful costume and coon concert in aid of th. Sick and Poor and Suuday School Funds of the Warrington Parish Church, was held in the Conservative Club Assembly Room on Thursday evening. Mrs. Ralph Johnson, who had arranged the concert, is to he con- gratulated upon theadmirable programme presented and , the manifest pleasure with which the efforts of all the . artistes were received. There was a crowded attendance . and encores were the order of the evening. The pro- , Igramme was delightfully varied and interesting throughout. All the performers wore very pretty costumes and when the chorus Was grouped on the platform a pictu.-esque scene was presented. The first part of the entertainment consisted of an enjoyable series of songs and choruses. There was a pleasing swing song by the Misses Pauline. and Mina Johnson, and Miss Dora Fairclough very sweetly sang " Land of Nod." A popular item was the " Song of Primroses " by the Misses Rose Wellhead, Dorothy Brown, Vida Whittle and Pauline Johnson. A song illustrating the pleasures and risks of a motor car journey proved highly amusing, the solo being admir- ably taken by Miss Adams. " A real town lady " was given with fine spirit by Nliss D umt, who later appeared In 3 military song and chorus which went with a capital swing and was one of the greatest successes of the evening. A Japanese tea song was given with good effect by the Misses Daunt, Well- head, Tunstall and Adams and a Chinese song was interpreted in a very tasteful manner by Miss Adams. The audience was highly delighted with two charming coon songs by Miss Rose Wallhead and Miss Brundritt and the accompanying dances were cleverly executed by these ladies and the members of the chorus. Mr, Maysmor' Harrison is an acco.r.Lalished whistles and his solos proved so acceptable that hearty encores were accorded. A one act play "The man in the street" (Louis N. Parker) was one of the chief attractions and it WAS presented in a most praiseworthy style by three well-known members of the Warrington Dramatic Society—Miss Margaret Pierpoint, Mr. Sidney Wall- head, and Mr. Harry Mosely. Each part was excel- lently sustained and the audience was loud in its appreciation. The duties of accompanist were dis- charged by Miss Evelyn Yonne. At the conclusion of the concert the assembly expressed its thanks to Mrs. Ralph Johnson for having arranged such a pleassnt evening's entertainment by giving three hearty cheers.

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Bishop —Butterworth.

A wedding of considerable local interest was celebrated at the New-street Wesleyan Church, Smeth- wick, on Thursday, the contracting parties being Miss Lucy Ellen Butterworth,(daughter of the late Mr. A. W. Butterworth,) and Mr. Kenneth Falls Bishop, (son of the Rev. Theodore Bishop of Gateshead and formerly of Warrington,) who is well known in Warrington as one of the engineers in charge at the electric lighting station, and as a prominent member of the Photo- graphic Society. There was a large congregation to witness the ceremony which was performed by the Revs. W. and H. V. Griffiths (uncle and cousin of the bride). The bride looked charming in a dress of ivory duchess satin with old lace trimming, and a white heather and orange blossom spray. She also wore a Brussels net veil surmounted by a wreath of orange blossom, and carried a beautiful shower bouquet of roses and lilies of the valley. The bridesmaids were the Misses Vera L. James, Marjory Lough and Doris 1 Lough (cousins of the bride) who were prettily gowned I in cream voile and satin with cream chiffon hats. Their bouquets consisted of roses and violets,the gifts of the bridegnom. In the absence, through illness of the bridegroom's brother, Mr. Ivor Lough James acted as best man. The service was fully choral, Mr. James Nicklin presiding at the organ and playing suitable selections, including the well-known wedding , marches. The reception was held at the house of the • bride's uncle after which the happy couple left for • Brocton where the honeymoon is being spent. Amongst the many beautiful presents which were received was a handsome antique clock from Mr. Bishop's fellow employes at the Warrington electric lighting station.

Gibson—Crozier.

'ln the ancient, and ivy-clad parish church of St. Cuthbert, Lytham, an interesting wedding was solem- nized on Wednesday afternoon, the contracting parties being Mr. Herbert Gibson, B.A. (youngest son of Mr. T. G. Gibmn, Warrington), and Miss Millicent Crozier, fifth daughter of Mr. Robert Crozier, of Clifton-square, Lytham.There was a large congregation at the church, Miss Cr )zier being well known and respected in the locality. The bride, who was given away by her father, was attired in a crearri cloth dress and Leghorn hat trimmed with chiffon, osprey and orange blossoms and carried a shower bouquet of white lilac and orange blossom. Her ornament was a 'diamond and sapphire ring,the gift of the bridegroom.Thebridesmaids were Miss Margaret Crozier (sister of the bride) and Miss Mary P. Gibson (sister of the bridegroom), and they wore brown chiffon, taffeta, and Leghorn hats trimmed with chiff in and roses, their shower bouquets being composed of Marechal Niel roses. Their orna- ments were gold and turquoise lockets the gift of the bridegroom.. The officiating clergyman was the Rev. ICanon Hawkins, M.A., vicar of St. Cuthbert, assisted by the Rev. Louis Ives, MA., of Lowestoft. Mr. Ernest S Kidd, M.A. of Egremont, was the best man. An admirable selection of music was played by Mr. S. H. Bronghton,Mus.l3aci. (Omon.), including the Grand March from" Lohengrin,"the "Spring song" (Hollins) the "Benediction " Nuptiale (Dubois), and" Fantasie Rustique" (Wolstenholme).
