THE NATIONALIZATION OF CANALS.

[BY CHARLES E. PARKER].

Members of Chambers of Commerce, and writers of articles in popular magazines, have for some time been directing the attention of the public to the question of the Canal System. Comparisons have been made between the mileage, the tonnage carried, and the money spent on the Continental canals, with the length, tonnage transported, and capital expenditure on English canals. The complaints have become so loud, the comparisons so striking, that the new Govern- ment has promised to appoint a Royal Commission to inquire into the whole subject. No doubt this Com- mission will weigh well all the evidence and very seriously consider the whole question before ' they recommend the country to any form of purchase or guarantee. The mistake that has been made in nationalizing and municipalizing commercial enter- prises ought to make us very careful before we under- take the responsibility of buying and working our inland waterways. The London County Council undertook with , a light heart the task of supplying a fleet of ferry steamers for the Thames at London ; the annual loss is about £50,000, which the ratepayers have to find each year. Comparisons are often very misleading, and it is important in making these to be sure that the condi- tions of the things we compare are essentially the same. In comparing the canals of the Continent and those of our own country, it is well to remember that in Northern Europe, where the canal system is most developed, they have a comparatively flat country, where for perhaps a stretch of a hundred miles not a single lock is required. Between Berlin and Hamburg, a distance of 230 miles, there are only three locks. Business of the Next Meeting. Mr. 'Minnie() who is 29 years of age, is an alumni of Nottingham University and the Midland Baptist College, the a/Ina mater of Drs. Clifford, Aked, Dawson, Burns, etc. Durihg his course at Nottingham he was awarded the Hewby Scholarship which is worth £5O a year. Ill- health, however, prevented him from taking full advan- tage of this. The rev. gentleman settled at Golborne- street in July 1902 and was ordained in October of the same year and amongst those who took part were— Professors Bumby, 8.A., Bowser, B.D. Revs. Dr. Aked, T. E. Roberts, M.A., H. V. Thomas and others. Letters , were also readifrom Drs. McLaren and Clifford. Since ; then Mr. Dunnico has won for himself a position of influence, and has gathered around him hosts of friends. ; His decision will be awaited with keen interest.

ARTHUR BENNETT. J. FULTON SMITH. TRAMWAY IN SANICRY-STRBIST.

A letter from the Warrington Tradesmen's Associa- tion asking the Committee to extend the tramways along Sankey-street to Legh-street was read at a meet- ing of the Tramways Committee. It was resolved that the Association be informed that the Corporation have no powers to make such extension. As the result of a petition it has also been resolved that tramwaymen and policemen when in uniform be allowed to travel on the cars free of charge.

. -- The Deputy Town Clerk reported to the General Pur- poses Committee that he had received the decision of the Attorney-General in reference to the application of the Council to institute proceedings against two mem- bers of the Council under the Public Bodies Corrupt Practices Act, HD ; that in one case the Attorney- General had consented to proceedings being taken, and that such proceedings would now be Laken.

The Liverpool Licensing Committee on Thursday- decided to sweep away 66 licences. Compensation will be paid, and some of the more costly houses may remain open for another year.

REV. HERBERT DUNNICO.

Important Charge Offered.

I The following announcement appeared in the " Liver- I pool Daily Post" :—The members of Kensington Baptist Church, Jubilee-drive, Liverpool, at a special church meeting held a few days ago, considered the re- commendations of the deacons as to the new minister to this church. There was a large attendance of members, and Mr. Lancaster, one of the deacons, presided. The church secretary (Mr. John Bennett) stated that he had convened that meeting according to the church con- stitution as to election of a pastor, and he would make known the decision of the Board, who, after prayerful consideration, asked the church members to give the Rev. Herbert Dunnico, of Warrington, a call to the pastorate, and that unanimously. There had been every opportunity I taken to hear good men, and, apart from the personal knowledge of Mr. Dunnico, and his power as a preacher sod lecturer, he was highly recommended by the Rev. 1 John Thomas, of Myrtle-street. Dr. Aked had also stated in a communication :—"Mr. Dunnico's coming to this city would be a great service to the Free Church movement, and to the cause of righteousness generally."—The members freely spoke in favour of the nomination, and each deacon added a word of testimony, land the call was given unanimously. I The Church at Kensington is one of the leading I churches in the denomination. The building has 1,200 sittings, the church roll contains between 500 and 600 names and there are nearly 900 scholars in the Sunday school. The pastorate has been vacant since December of last year when the Rev. E. E. Walter ex-moderator lof the Union retired after a pastorate of 34 year. The call was given unanimously at one of the largest church meetings ever held. A large number of congratulatory letters have been sent urging Mr. Dunnico to accept. Dr. Aked says "I am very happy in the prospect of haviry, you for a neighbour, a colleague and friend near aChand. Your coming to Liverpool will bring me a personal pleasure as well as worlds of help to good causes. It is a great thing for you to come to Liverpool so early, it is a fine opening, the prospects are exceedingly bright, founda- tions good, and you will have a good career. I rejoice in your prospects, for your own sake and that of all the movements with which my life is bound." Prior to the sermon on Sunday evening Mr. Dunnico intimated to the crowded congregation that he had received a unanimous call to the pastorate at Liverpool. They would recognize at once that such an important offer demanded the most careful and prayerfnl consideration but he hoped to be able to announce his decision on Sunday next..

WARRINGTON WORKS COMPETITION.

The final in connection with the Warrington Works Competition will be decided this (Friday)evening. The progress of the contest has been fellowed with 'great interest and there is every prospect of the final pro- ducing as keen a struggle as can be wished. In it Fletchers, the holders of the championship are to meet Crosfleld's who have shown excellent form, and it is didcult to say which team vill be victorious. At the conclusion of the game Alderman Tinnion will present the medals to the winners and the runners-up and Mr. J. E. Warren will present the medals and cup won in connection with the Warrington Junior League. The teams fen t,,night's match are : Crostlelds :W. Lawton, full back; Shipley, Tinsley, Leah and Dun- bobbin, three-quarters ; Stokes and Gaylen, halves ; Ray, Caldwell, Bradihaw, Elwell and [toylance, forwards (to be selected from). Fletcher's : Mitchell, full back; flelbrnok, Grainger P. Knight and Isherwood threequarters: Kay and Clegg, , halves Boardman, Holbrook, Hardy, Melton and Clare, forwards. The semi-finals on Saturday last attracted a large, crowd to the Widerspool ground and some excellent I football was witnessed. _

Fletchers and the Locos had an exceedingly stiff fight and only one point separated the teams when time was called, the score being Fletchers 7 points Locos 6. Crosfield's had a much lightertask in their match with Greenings and won by 11 points to nil.

TOWN COUNCIL.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

===== 0002601_19060428_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
By " Ovalist."

The football season terminates to-day (Saturday), and as their concluding match Warrington have a very interesting fixture. For months the Leigh and Hunslet clubs have been running a neck and neck race with each other for the championship of the Northern Union League. First one has led and then the other and to-day only a small percentage divides the two clubs. Both have a concluding engagement -- Hunslet at Warrington, and Leigh at Barrow —and the result of these matches will determine the championship. The odds are very much on Leigh at present and the only chance of any other result is for Hunslet to win at Warrington;and for Leigh to either lose or draw at Barrow.

Our oflice mathematician has been calculating the percentages for Me under all the possible circum- stances, and the figures work out so close that he got quite excited over the task, notwithStanding the violent headache brought on by the strain of complicated calculation.

Herewith I append the final percentages of the two clubs under all possible circumstances. Present position : Leigh Hunslet If both lose to-day the percentages will be : Leigh Hunslet If both win: Leigh Hunslet If both draw: Leigh Hunslet If Hunslet win and Leigh draw or lose : Hunslet Leigh(a) Leigh (/) 78.20

Of course, if Hunslet lose, Leigh win whatever the result of their match with Barrow may be.

Many of my readers are puzzled as to the way to calculate the percentages of results in the Northern League. Ili s perfectly simple " when ye know." Thus : Leigh have played 29 matches, won 23, drawn I—baking 47 points out of a possible 58. Add a couple of cyphers to the 47, making 4700, and divide by the 53, and you get the percentage, 81.02. No charge, gentlemen, for the information unless you have borrowed your" Examiner " instead of buying it. In that case you owe us one penny.

The Warrington men will make a determined effort to-day to keep the Championship in Lancashire, and unless Hunslet produce rather more than their best form they will lose. The following team has been chosen to represent Warrington : Berry, full back: Fish, Dickenson, Taylor, and Jordan threegnarters; Lees and Brookes, half-backs ; Swift, Thomas, Shu,gars,Heath, Boardman, Preston, Harmer and Belton, forwards.

Cycling. The club hitherto known as the Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Cycling Club has been reorganized and taken over by the Wesley Guild. There is a large and active membership and a successful season is confidently anticipated. The officers are :—President, Mr. James Dodds ; vice-presi dent, Mr. C. Bromley ; captain, Mr. A. Blythe ; sub-captains, Mr. A. Highley and Mr. R. Sudlow ; hon. treasurer, Miss L. Hardy; hon. secretary, Mr. J. Webb ; committee, Messrs. A. Blythe, A.Highley, C. Pritchard, J. Harding, and C. Bromley. The follow- ing is the list of fixtures : May 5 Lymm. 12 Knutsford. 19 Bedford Leigh. 26 Arley Mill. June 2 Pickmere. B. H. 4 Southport. 9 Sandiway. 16 Frodsham. 23 Rostherne Mere. 30 Open. July 7 Altrincbam. 14 PiamerA. 21 Castle Mill. 28 Arley Mill. Aug. 4 Hale. B.H. 6 Chester. 11 Knutsford. 18 Liverpool. 25 Frodsham. Sept. 1 Piekmere. 8 Altrincharu. 15 Arley Mill. 22 Great Budworth. 29 Daresbury via Whitley. -- Cricket.

The Warrington Cricket Club has just taken a step which should do a great deal to increase local enthusiasm in the summer sport. At the weekly meet- ing of the club on Monday, it was decided to provido gold medals for a competition for the whole of the works clubs within the borough. This generous offer ought to call forth a hearty response, and the club is deserving of cordial thanks for this effort to encourage the game. Of course much preliminary work will IMve to be done before the competition can be got into thorough working order, and for this object a meeting of the various works representatives desirous of taking part will be held at the Arpley pavilion on Monday evening next at 7.30. There is every probability that the project will be entered into with spiritand that :1 keen competition will be witnessed. The splendid ground at Arpley will he at the disposal of the clubs, and the matches will take place on Priday evenings, on exactly similar lines to those which govern like competitions in the whole of East Lanca- shire. In some parts the contests are worked on league principles whilst in others the knock-out rule is in vogue. principles, side is allowed to ha an hour, so that naturally there is some exciting play. A small charge is made for admission, and by this m ans a fund is raised for the working of the competition. All these details, of course, remain to be settled but there seems to be every reason to believe that Warrington will ere long boast a league competition which will compare well with similar ventures in the county.

Warrington should have played their opening match with Ashton-under-Lyne on Saturday, hut rain fell so heavily that the fixture had to be cancelled.

Sunday School Anniversary.

Tho Sunday School anniversary in connection with, Ffill Cliff° Baptist Church, was held on Sunday when the preacher for the occasion was the Rev. P. Cowell- Lloyd, of Altrincham. In the morning Mr. Lloyd spoke from the words" And a little child shall lead them." In the afternoon a musical service was held a good attendance being presided over by Mr. Thomas Deakin. Special hymns were sung by the sch oiars. A violin solo was effectually rendered by Mrs. Haviour and a helpful and instructive address given by ,Mr. George Brown on " Cups of cold water." In the evening the chapel was filled. Mr. Lloyd based a forcible appeal upon the subject, "An unwelcome Christ." A special feature at all the services was the singing of Miss 'Towena Thomas, of London, and in the morning she contributed "Homeland " with feeling. In the after- noon the congregation were treated to a very excellent rendering of" Come unto [Ern " (" Messiah ") and at the evening service Miss Thomas sang "Nearer my God to Thee " most effectively. At both afternoon and evening services the choir acquitted themselves very creditably in the anthem " Suffer the children." Miss Jones, Mr. James Strange and Mr. Muir acted as organ- ist during the day. The collections realized £5 10s. 6d.

The Education Bill and Religious Liberty. Preaching at Latchford Baptist Church on Suneay evening the Rev. J. F. Matthews took as his subject "The Education Bill and Religious Liberty." Address- ing a large congregation he selected as his text"And;the chief captain answered : With a great sum obtained I this freedom. And Paul said, But I was free born." Acts xxii. 28. As the revised version had it, Paul said "I am a Roman born" and when he said that he declared himself to be the heir of all privileges which the Roman Empire conferred upon free citizens. A Jew in extraction and a Greek by birth St. Paul often found it necessary, not for his osm sake but often for others, to assert his legal rights. Often in later ages when men had been fighting for civil and religious liberty they had had to take their stand upon some right which had already been conferred upon them,. That had been especially the case in the growth of civil and religious freedom in this country. The country since the advent of Christianity had had to assert and re-assert its independence of the ecclesiastical jurisdic- tion of Rome. Rome had laid her hand upon all coun- tries of the West and had claimed that as religious monarch her chief pastor yielded no precedent to any monarch. The Church had demanded allegiance from all the faithful and it was easy to perceive how religious conflicts had arisen when allegiance to the Papacy had clashed with allegiance to the constitu- • tion. One line upon which we had had to fight for our freedom had been the constant assertion of the inalienable right of the individual te think for himself, to speak for himself, and to worship as his , conscience dictated. Very often interference with the latter privilege had been bound up with interference ' with the civil rights of the individual and of the nation. I That was conspicuously apparent in the contest between 4 the people of England and the Stuarts. The climax was reached when the head of Charles I. fell from the block and the people felt that it was never to be enacted over again. Unfortunately during the last ten years we had had to fight a not dissimilar battle for civil and religious freedom. That battle was not yet finished, in fact one could almost say it had scarcely begun. Within the last month an Education Bill—so-called —had been introduced into the House of Commons That Bill meant three things possible. Everything in the nature of sectarian teaching was banished from the precints of the school during legal school hours. Secondly a tea3her would be submitted to no religious test and need not teach any form of religion unless he chose to do so, and thirdly it followed tlt t 41 head teacherships were open to men and women of lirains and character. It son unfortunate that the educational system of this countrl in its bearing upon the primary schools should still be., the battle ground for religious freedom awl it seemed to him that if the Bill was not passed _ . in its present form but in a form In which the four-fifths principle was considerably modified, it would still further increase the measure ' of religions freedom which we enjoyed. Why men or women who became civil servants should have to so mit to some religions test before they could take the' proper place in the school buildings he could not : understand. Nor was that all. Hitherto, except with certain reservations, subject to the protection of the conscience clause people had had to send their children to day schools where they had been taught scripture , lessons in the school time. That was done away with by the new Bill. If the Bill passed no parent was under any ' obligation to send his child to school before the secular instruction began. He quite admitted that to ' many good people of all churches and all creeds, the in- icreasing responsibility for the training of the child heart in religious knowledge was of appalling solemnity The fact nevertheless remained that the home, the Church and Sunday School would become more and more the best organizations for that special purpose. Personally he hoped that before the Bill was registered on the statute book of the realm nothing in the nature of religious teaching would be allowed to be given by any- 'One connected with the day schools within school hours. If that came to pass the primary schools would cease to be the battle ground of religious controversy. True the controversy would be shifted and probably sooner than some imagined. True it would takeanother form but it could only have one end—the complete emancipation or religion from state patronage and control, the final abolition of the ridiculous and silly oath which the Ring had to take upon his coronation and the complete enthronization of Christ Jesus as the head of His own creation.

The Amalgamated Union of Musicians has a grievance. Army, navy and police bandsmen, it is stated, are in the habit of competing with civiliaft musicians at" unfair rates " and a petition against the practice is to be presented to the Army Council.

The body was taken to the Padgate Stocks to await the inquest. Death of Miss E. Dann. We record with rep et the death, which took plane on Tuesday, at Moss House, Whitefield-road, Stockton Heath, of Miss Ethel Dunn daughter of the late Mr- A. H. Dunn, after a very brief illness. Miss Dunn; who was 22 years of age, was formerly an assistant teacher at Wycliffe schools, but left Warrington to take up a post at Earlestown. About a fortnight ago she was seized with an attack of rheumatic fever, and despite medical aid passed away as above stated. A singer of considerable ability, she was for many years a member of\ the choir of Bold-street Wesleyan Church, and she also figured with success at a number of concerts in con- nection with the Church.—The funeral took place at Hill Cliffe on Thursday afteruoon the officiating minister being the Rev. J. Manning. In addition to the relatives many friends were at the graveside. PUBLIC BENEFIT.—IINDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Beautiful Warrington Society. Great interest is being taken in the efforts of thi society to beautify the town, and with the object o prbmoting the objects of the organization in Bewsey Ward a meeting was held in Hoyle-street mission on Friday evening. Dr. Adams presided, and Ur. Darlington undertook the duties of hon. secretary pro tern. The chairman remarked that even smoky Bewsey might be made attractive by trees and window- boxes and other decorations and he hoped the inhabit- ants would do all they could to assist the society in making that part of Warrington as beautiful as possible. Mr. J. Fairhurst and Mr. S. Barlow both strongly urged the desirability of beautifying the ward. A strong committee was formed and there is every prospect that the work will be taken up with enthu- siasm in the neighlsourhood.

PADGATE. _— PARISH COUNClL.—Theibannual meeting of the Poulton-with-Fearnhead Parish Council was held in the National School the other evening. There were present --Dr. J. Bennett, Messrs. Richard Denham. G. Walker, James Houghton, J. Gandy and the Clerk (Mr. F. Wright). Dr. Bennett was unanimously elected chair- man, Mr. R. Denham was chosen as vice-chair)nan,while Messrs. Walker, Denham and J. Houghton were appointed overseers. Messrs. Denham, Walker and Dr. Bennett were appointed Finance Committee.—The Chairman reported that he had interviewed the postal authorities regarding the placing of a letter-box near the Wesleyan Church and that the Clerk had received a communication saying that the matter was receiving consideration.
